	</label>

<div class="sidebar-list mw-collapsible mw-collapsed"><div class="sidebar-list-title" style="background:transparent;border-top:1px solid #aaa;text-align:center;">Related topics</div><div class="sidebar-list-content mw-collapsible-content hlist">
<ul><li>Liberal Party factions

<li><a href="/wiki/Politics_of_Australia" title="Politics of Australia">Politics of Australia</a></li></ul></div></div></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-below plainlist">
<ul><li><span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg/15px-DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg.png" decoding="async" width="15" height="16" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg/22px-DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg/30px-DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="249" data-file-height="268" /></span></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Conservatism" title="Portal:Conservatism">Conservatism&#32;portal</a></li>
<li><span class="mw-image-border noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="flag" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b9/Flag_of_Australia.svg/16px-Flag_of_Australia.svg.png" decoding="async" width="16" height="8" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b9/Flag_of_Australia.svg/24px-Flag_of_Australia.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b9/Flag_of_Australia.svg/32px-Flag_of_Australia.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="640" /></span></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Australia" title="Portal:Australia">Australia&#32;portal</a></li></ul></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Anthony John Abbott</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Companion_of_the_Order_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Companion of the Order of Australia">AC</a></span> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="/æ/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;bad&#39;">æ</span><span title="&#39;b&#39; in &#39;buy&#39;">b</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span><span title="&#39;t&#39; in &#39;tie&#39;">t</span></span>/</a></span></span>;<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> born 4 November 1957) is an Australian former politician who served as the 28th <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Australia">prime minister of Australia</a> from 2013 to 2015. He held office as the leader of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia" title="Liberal Party of Australia">Liberal Party of Australia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-aphbio_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-aphbio-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Abbott was born in London, England, to an Australian mother and a British father, and moved to Sydney at the age of two. He studied economics and law at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Sydney" title="University of Sydney">University of Sydney</a>, and then attended <a href="/wiki/The_Queen%27s_College,_Oxford" title="The Queen&#39;s College, Oxford">The Queen's College, Oxford</a>, as a <a href="/wiki/Rhodes_Scholar" class="mw-redirect" title="Rhodes Scholar">Rhodes Scholar</a>, studying <a href="/wiki/Philosophy,_Politics_and_Economics" class="mw-redirect" title="Philosophy, Politics and Economics">Philosophy, Politics and Economics</a>. After graduating from Oxford, Abbott briefly trained as a Roman Catholic <a href="/wiki/Seminarian" class="mw-redirect" title="Seminarian">seminarian</a>, and later worked as a journalist, manager, and political adviser. In 1992, he was appointed director of <a href="/wiki/Australians_for_Constitutional_Monarchy" title="Australians for Constitutional Monarchy">Australians for Constitutional Monarchy</a>, a position he held until his election to parliament as a <a href="/wiki/Member_of_parliament" title="Member of parliament">member of parliament</a> (MP) for the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Warringah" title="Division of Warringah">division of Warringah</a> at the <a href="/wiki/1994_Warringah_by-election" title="1994 Warringah by-election">1994 Warringah by-election</a>, before the election of the <a href="/wiki/Howard_government" title="Howard government">Howard government</a> in 1996.
</p><p>Following the <a href="/wiki/1998_Australian_federal_election" title="1998 Australian federal election">1998 election</a>, Abbott was appointed <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Employment_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Employment (Australia)">Minister for Employment Services</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Second_Howard_ministry" title="Second Howard ministry">second Howard ministry</a>. He was promoted to <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_Australia" title="Cabinet of Australia">cabinet</a> in 2001 as <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Employment_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Employment (Australia)">Minister for Employment, Workplace Relations and Small Business</a>. In 2003, Abbott became <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Health_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Health (Australia)">Minister for Health and Ageing</a>, retaining this position until the defeat of the <a href="/wiki/Howard_government" title="Howard government">Howard government</a> at the <a href="/wiki/2007_Australian_federal_election" title="2007 Australian federal election">2007 election</a>. Initially serving in the shadow cabinets of <a href="/wiki/Brendan_Nelson" title="Brendan Nelson">Brendan Nelson</a> and then <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Turnbull" title="Malcolm Turnbull">Malcolm Turnbull</a>, Abbott resigned from the front bench in November 2009, in protest against Turnbull's support for the <a href="/wiki/Rudd_government_(2007%E2%80%9310)" class="mw-redirect" title="Rudd government (2007–10)">Rudd government</a>'s proposed <a href="/wiki/Emissions_Trading_Scheme" class="mw-redirect" title="Emissions Trading Scheme">Emissions Trading Scheme</a> (ETS).<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Forcing a <a href="/wiki/2009_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill" title="2009 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill">leadership ballot</a> on the subject, Abbott narrowly defeated Turnbull to become the party's leader and <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(Australia)" title="Leader of the Opposition (Australia)">leader of the opposition</a>. Abbott led the <a href="/wiki/Coalition_(Australia)" title="Coalition (Australia)">Liberal-National Coalition</a> to the <a href="/wiki/2010_Australian_federal_election" title="2010 Australian federal election">2010 federal election</a>, which resulted in a <a href="/wiki/Hung_parliament" title="Hung parliament">hung parliament</a>, and an eventual victory for the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Australian Labor Party</a> (ALP).<sup id="cite_ref-curtis1_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-curtis1-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Abbott remained leader, and led the Coalition to a <a href="/wiki/Landslide_victory" title="Landslide victory">landslide victory</a> at the <a href="/wiki/2013_Australian_federal_election" title="2013 Australian federal election">2013 election</a>.
</p><p>After assuming office, the <a href="/wiki/Abbott_government" title="Abbott government">Abbott government</a> implemented <a href="/wiki/Operation_Sovereign_Borders" title="Operation Sovereign Borders">Operation Sovereign Borders</a> in an effort to halt illegal maritime arrivals.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> It abolished several reforms enacted by the preceding government, including the <a href="/wiki/Minerals_Resource_Rent_Tax" title="Minerals Resource Rent Tax">Minerals Resource Rent Tax</a> and <a href="/wiki/Carbon_pricing_in_Australia" title="Carbon pricing in Australia">Australia's carbon pricing scheme</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> His government aimed to rein in a <a href="/wiki/Australian_federal_budget" title="Australian federal budget">federal budget</a> deficit that reached <a href="/wiki/Australian_dollar" title="Australian dollar">A$</a>48.5&#160;billion by June 2014,<sup id="cite_ref-bloomberg.com_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bloomberg.com-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> and established the <a href="/wiki/National_Commission_of_Audit" title="National Commission of Audit">National Commission of Audit</a> to advise on restoring the federal budget to surplus. Abbott instituted the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Commission_into_Trade_Union_Governance_and_Corruption" title="Royal Commission into Trade Union Governance and Corruption">Royal Commission into Trade Union Governance and Corruption</a>; founded the <a href="/wiki/Medical_Research_Future_Fund" title="Medical Research Future Fund">Medical Research Future Fund</a>; and produced <a href="/wiki/White_paper" title="White paper">white papers</a> on <a href="/wiki/White_Paper_on_Developing_Northern_Australia" title="White Paper on Developing Northern Australia">developing Northern Australia</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Agricultural_Competitiveness_White_Paper" title="Agricultural Competitiveness White Paper">Agricultural Competitiveness</a>. In international affairs, Abbott concluded free trade agreements with China, Japan and South Korea. He challenged the Russian president <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Putin" title="Vladimir Putin">Vladimir Putin</a> over Russia's actions in Ukraine and over the shooting down of <a href="/wiki/Malaysia_Airlines_Flight_17" title="Malaysia Airlines Flight 17">Malaysian Flight MH17</a> in Ukraine. He committed Australian forces to the battle against <a href="/wiki/ISIS" class="mw-redirect" title="ISIS">ISIS</a> during the <a href="/wiki/Syrian_civil_war" title="Syrian civil war">Syrian conflict</a>, and agreed to resettle an additional 12,000 refugees from the region.<sup id="cite_ref-ABCSyriaairstrikesrefugees_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ABCSyriaairstrikesrefugees-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> He launched the <a href="/wiki/New_Colombo_Plan" title="New Colombo Plan">New Colombo Plan</a> to encourage educational exchange with the Indo-Pacific region. Domestically, Abbott campaigned for recognition of <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_Australians" title="Indigenous Australians">Indigenous Australians</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Constitution">Australian Constitution</a>, and promised a plebiscite on the issue of <a href="/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_Australia" title="Same-sex marriage in Australia">same-sex marriage</a>.
</p><p>Abbott's "budget repair" measures proved unpopular, with his government's austere <a href="/wiki/2014_Australian_federal_budget" title="2014 Australian federal budget">2014 budget</a> being widely criticised.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Due to Abbott's poor opinion polling and personal unpopularity, he was defeated by rival Malcolm Turnbull in a <a href="/wiki/September_2015_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill" title="September 2015 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill">September 2015 leadership spill</a>, and replaced as prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> He remained in the Parliament as a <a href="/wiki/Backbencher" title="Backbencher">backbencher</a>, until he lost his seat of Warringah to independent candidate <a href="/wiki/Zali_Steggall" title="Zali Steggall">Zali Steggall</a> at the <a href="/wiki/2019_Australian_federal_election" title="2019 Australian federal election">2019 federal election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> In September 2020, he was named an adviser to the British government's <a href="/wiki/Board_of_Trade" title="Board of Trade">Board of Trade</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-https&#58;//www.gov.uk/government/groups/board-of-trade#advisers-to-the-board-september-2020_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-https://www.gov.uk/government/groups/board-of-trade#advisers-to-the-board-september-2020-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Abbott continues to contribute to international public debate as a writer, public speaker and advocate for conservative causes. He is often ranked in the <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_prime_ministers_of_Australia" title="Historical rankings of prime ministers of Australia">lower tier</a> of Australian prime ministers.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Birth_and_family_background">Birth and family background</span></h3>
<p>Abbott was born on 4 November 1957 at the <a href="/wiki/General_Lying-In_Hospital" title="General Lying-In Hospital">General Lying-In Hospital</a> in <a href="/wiki/Lambeth" title="Lambeth">Lambeth</a>, London, England. He is the oldest of four children born to Fay (née Peters; b. 1933) and Richard Henry "Dick" Abbott (1924–2017).<sup id="cite_ref-dailytelegraph.com.au_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dailytelegraph.com.au-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> He has three younger sisters, including <a href="/wiki/Christine_Forster" title="Christine Forster">Christine Forster</a>, who has also been involved in politics.<sup id="cite_ref-pete_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pete-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> His mother was born in Sydney,<sup id="cite_ref-pete_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pete-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> while his father was born in <a href="/wiki/Newcastle_upon_Tyne" title="Newcastle upon Tyne">Newcastle upon Tyne</a>, England.<sup id="cite_ref-dailytelegraph.com.au_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dailytelegraph.com.au-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At age 16, Dick Abbott moved to Australia with his parents.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Two years later, in 1942, he was called up to the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Australian_Air_Force" title="Royal Australian Air Force">Royal Australian Air Force</a>. Dick Abbott and his mother returned to the UK in 1954 where he met and married Fay Peters, a dietitian.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Childhood_and_education">Childhood and education</span></h3>
<p>On 7 September 1960, Abbott, his parents, and younger sister Jane, left the UK for Australia on the <a href="/wiki/Assisted_Passage_Migration_Scheme" class="mw-redirect" title="Assisted Passage Migration Scheme">Assisted Passage Migration Scheme</a> ship <a href="/wiki/SS_Oronsay_(1950)" title="SS Oronsay (1950)">SS <i>Oronsay</i></a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Oronsay_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Oronsay-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-north_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-north-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Settling in Sydney, the family first lived in the suburb of <a href="/wiki/Bronte,_New_South_Wales" title="Bronte, New South Wales">Bronte</a> and later moved to <a href="/wiki/Chatswood,_New_South_Wales" title="Chatswood, New South Wales">Chatswood</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-AFRAbbottGillard_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AFRAbbottGillard-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Dick Abbott established what was to become one of the largest <a href="/wiki/Orthodontics" title="Orthodontics">orthodontics</a> practices in Australia, retiring in 2002.<sup id="cite_ref-north_24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-north-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Abbott attended primary school at <a href="/wiki/St_Aloysius%27_College_(Sydney)" title="St Aloysius&#39; College (Sydney)">St Aloysius' College</a> at <a href="/wiki/Milsons_Point,_New_South_Wales" title="Milsons Point, New South Wales">Milson's Point</a>, before completing his secondary school education at <a href="/wiki/Saint_Ignatius%27_College,_Riverview" title="Saint Ignatius&#39; College, Riverview">St Ignatius' College, Riverview</a>, both <a href="/wiki/Jesuit" class="mw-redirect" title="Jesuit">Jesuit</a> schools.<sup id="cite_ref-facts_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-facts-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> During his time at St Ignatius' College, one was his teachers was <a href="/wiki/John_Kennedy_(Australian_politician)" title="John Kennedy (Australian politician)">John Kennedy</a>, who would later go on to serve as the member for <a href="/wiki/Electoral_district_of_Hawthorn" title="Electoral district of Hawthorn">Hawthorn</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> He graduated with a Bachelor of Economics (BEc) in 1979 and a Bachelor of Laws (LLB) in 1981<sup id="cite_ref-aphbio_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-aphbio-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> from the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Sydney" title="University of Sydney">University of Sydney</a>. He resided at <a href="/wiki/St_John%27s_College,_University_of_Sydney" title="St John&#39;s College, University of Sydney">St John's College</a> and was president of the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Sydney_Students%27_Representative_Council" title="University of Sydney Students&#39; Representative Council">Student Representative Council</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-dailytelegraph1_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dailytelegraph1-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Influenced by his chaplain at St Ignatius', Father Emmet Costello, he then attended <a href="/wiki/The_Queen%27s_College,_Oxford" title="The Queen&#39;s College, Oxford">The Queen's College, Oxford</a>, as a <a href="/wiki/Rhodes_Scholar" class="mw-redirect" title="Rhodes Scholar">Rhodes Scholar</a>, where in June 1983 he graduated as a Bachelor of Arts in <a href="/wiki/Philosophy,_Politics_and_Economics" class="mw-redirect" title="Philosophy, Politics and Economics">Philosophy, Politics and Economics</a> (PPE) and on 21 October 1989 proceeded by seniority to <a href="/wiki/Master_of_Arts_(Oxbridge_and_Dublin)" class="mw-redirect" title="Master of Arts (Oxbridge and Dublin)">Master of Arts</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-kenny_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kenny-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During his university days, Abbott gained media attention for political opposition to the then dominant left-wing student leadership. Once he was violently beaten at a university conference.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated3_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated3-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> A student newspaper editor with political views opposed to those of Abbott took him to court for indecent assault after he touched her during a student debate; the charges were dismissed by the court.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> According to the <i><a href="/wiki/The_Sun-Herald" title="The Sun-Herald">Sun-Herald</a></i> newspaper, it was "an ugly and often violent time", and Abbott's tactics in student politics were like "an aggressive terrier".<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Abbott organised rallies in support of <a href="/wiki/Governor-General_of_Australia" title="Governor-General of Australia">Governor-General</a> <a href="/wiki/John_Kerr_(governor-general)" title="John Kerr (governor-general)">John Kerr</a> after he <a href="/wiki/1975_Australian_constitutional_crisis" title="1975 Australian constitutional crisis">dismissed the Whitlam government</a> in November 1975, as well as a pro-<a href="/wiki/Falklands_War" title="Falklands War">Falklands War</a> demonstration during his time at Oxford.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> At St. Ignatius College, Abbott had been taught and influenced by the <a href="/wiki/Society_of_Jesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Society of Jesus">Jesuits</a>. At university, he encountered <a href="/wiki/B._A._Santamaria" title="B. A. Santamaria">B. A. Santamaria</a>, a Catholic layman who led a movement against Communism within the Australian labour movement in the 1950s, culminating in the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party_split_of_1955" title="Australian Labor Party split of 1955">1955 Labor Party split</a> and the formation of the <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Labor_Party_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Democratic Labor Party (Australia)">Democratic Labor Party</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated3_34-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated3-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Santamaria has been described as Abbott's "political hero". He wrote the foreword to a novelisation of Santamaria's life written by <a href="/wiki/Alan_Reid_(journalist)" title="Alan Reid (journalist)">Alan Reid</a>, and in 2015 launched a biography of Santamaria written by <a href="/wiki/Gerard_Henderson" title="Gerard Henderson">Gerard Henderson</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> In 1977, Abbott faced charges of <a href="/wiki/Common_assault" title="Common assault">common</a> and <a href="/wiki/Indecent_assault" title="Indecent assault">indecent assault</a> after allegedly groping trainee teacher Helen Wilson while she was making a speech at the Kuring-gai College of Advanced Education in Sydney. Abbott pleaded not guilty, and the charges were ultimately dropped.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarr201224_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarr201224-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Abbott was a student boxer, earning two <a href="/wiki/Blue_(university_sport)" title="Blue (university sport)">Blues</a> for boxing while at Oxford.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> He was a heavyweight with modest height and reach.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When Abbott was a student, on one occasion he rescued a child who had been pulled out into the sea by the current. On another occasion, while drinking at a pub, he helped rescue children from the burning house next door. On both of these occasions, he left the scene after the rescues and did not wait to be thanked.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_adult_life_and_pre-political_career">Early adult life and pre-political career</span></h3>
<p>Following his time in Britain, Abbott returned to Australia and told his family of his intention to join the priesthood. In 1984 at the age of 26, he entered <a href="/wiki/St_Patrick%27s_Seminary" title="St Patrick&#39;s Seminary">St Patrick's Seminary</a>, Manly.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated3_34-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated3-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Abbott did not complete his studies at the seminary, leaving the institution in 1987. Interviewed before the 2013 election, Abbott said of his time as a trainee priest: "The Jesuits had helped to instil in me this thought that our calling in life was to be, to use the phrase: 'a man for others'. And I thought then that the best way in which I could be a 'man for others' was to become a priest. I discovered pretty soon that I was a bit of a square peg in a round hole … eventually working out that, I'm afraid, I just didn't have what it took to be an effective priest."<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated7_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated7-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Abbott worked in journalism, briefly ran a concrete plant, and began to get involved in national politics.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated3_34-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated3-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Throughout his time as a student and seminarian, he was writing articles for newspapers and magazines—first for <i><a href="/wiki/Honi_Soit" title="Honi Soit">Honi Soit</a></i> (the University of Sydney student newspaper) and later <i><a href="/wiki/The_Catholic_Weekly" title="The Catholic Weekly">The Catholic Weekly</a></i> and national publications such as <i><a href="/wiki/The_Bulletin_(Australian_periodical)" title="The Bulletin (Australian periodical)">The Bulletin</a></i>. He eventually became a journalist and wrote for <i><a href="/wiki/The_Australian" title="The Australian">The Australian</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-dailytelegraph1_29-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dailytelegraph1-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At birth, Abbott was a British citizen<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> by birth in the UK and by descent from his British-born father. He did not hold Australian citizenship from birth, as at the time Australian citizenship by descent could only be acquired from the father.  Abbott became a naturalised Australian citizen on 26 June 1981, apparently so as to become eligible for a Rhodes scholarship.  On 12 October 1993, he renounced his British citizenship to be eligible to run for parliament under <a href="/wiki/Section_44_of_the_Constitution_of_Australia" title="Section 44 of the Constitution of Australia">section 44 of the constitution</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_career">Political career</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_career">Early career</span></h3>
<p>Abbott began his public life when he was employed as a journalist for <i>The Bulletin</i>, an influential news magazine, and later for <i>The Australian</i> newspaper.<sup id="cite_ref-dailytelegraph1_29-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dailytelegraph1-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> While deciding his future career path, Abbott developed friendships with senior figures in the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party_(New_South_Wales_Branch)" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Labor Party (New South Wales Branch)">New South Wales Labor Party</a>, and was encouraged by <a href="/wiki/Bob_Carr" title="Bob Carr">Bob Carr</a>, as well as <a href="/wiki/Johno_Johnson" title="Johno Johnson">Johno Johnson</a>, to join the Labor Party and run for office. Abbott felt uncomfortable with the role of unions within the party, however, and wrote in his biography that he felt Labor "just wasn't the party for me."<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> For a time he worked as a plant manager for Pioneer Concrete before becoming press secretary to <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia" title="Liberal Party of Australia">Liberal Leader</a> <a href="/wiki/John_Hewson" title="John Hewson">John Hewson</a> from 1990 to 1993, helping to develop the <a href="/wiki/Fightback!_(policy)" title="Fightback! (policy)">Fightback!</a> policy.<sup id="cite_ref-dailytelegraph1_29-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dailytelegraph1-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/John_Howard" title="John Howard">John Howard</a> wrote in his autobiography that Abbott considered working on his staff before accepting the position with <i>The Bulletin</i>, and it was on Howard's recommendation that Hewson engaged Abbott. According to Howard, he and Abbott established a good rapport, but Hewson and Abbott fell out shortly before the <a href="/wiki/1993_Australian_federal_election" title="1993 Australian federal election">1993 election</a>, and Abbott ended up in search of work following the re-election of the <a href="/wiki/Keating_government" title="Keating government">Keating government</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> He was approached to head <a href="/wiki/Australians_for_Constitutional_Monarchy" title="Australians for Constitutional Monarchy">Australians for Constitutional Monarchy</a> (ACM), the main group organising support for the maintenance of the <a href="/wiki/Monarchy_in_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Monarchy in Australia">Monarchy in Australia</a> amidst the Keating government's campaign for a change to a republic.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2_52-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Abbott renounced his British citizenship in 1993.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Between 1993 and 1994, Abbott was Executive Director of ACM.<sup id="cite_ref-aphbio_4-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-aphbio-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> According to biographer Michael Duffy, Abbott's involvement with ACM "strengthened his relationship with John Howard, who in 1994 suggested he seek pre-selection for a by-election in the seat of Warringah".<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Howard provided a glowing reference and Abbott won pre-selection for the safe Liberal seat.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite his conservative leanings, Abbott acknowledged he voted for Labor in the <a href="/wiki/1988_New_South_Wales_state_election" title="1988 New South Wales state election">1988 NSW state election</a> as he thought that "<a href="/wiki/Barrie_Unsworth" title="Barrie Unsworth">Barrie Unsworth</a> was the best deal Premier that New South Wales had ever had". Nevertheless, Abbott then clarified that he has never voted for Labor in a federal election.<sup id="cite_ref-Attard2005-06-12_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Attard2005-06-12-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Member_of_Parliament.2C_1994.E2.80.932009"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Member_of_Parliament,_1994–2009">Member of Parliament, 1994–2009</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tony_Abbott.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Tony_Abbott.jpg/170px-Tony_Abbott.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="213" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/96/Tony_Abbott.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="200" data-file-height="250" /></a><figcaption>Tony Abbott in 1996</figcaption></figure>
<p>Abbott won Liberal preselection for the federal <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Warringah" title="Division of Warringah">Division of Warringah</a> <a href="/wiki/1994_Warringah_by-election" title="1994 Warringah by-election">by-election in March 1994</a> following the resignation of <a href="/wiki/Michael_MacKellar" title="Michael MacKellar">Michael MacKellar</a>.  He easily held the safe Liberal seat in the Liberals' traditional <a href="/wiki/Northern_Beaches" title="Northern Beaches">Northern Beaches</a> heartland, suffering a swing of only 1 percentage point in the primary vote.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> He easily won the seat in his own right at the <a href="/wiki/1996_Australian_federal_election" title="1996 Australian federal election">1996 general election</a>. Before 2019, he only dropped below 59 percent of the two-party vote once, in <a href="/wiki/2001_Australian_federal_election" title="2001 Australian federal election">2001</a>; that year independent <a href="/wiki/Peter_Macdonald_(Australian_politician)" title="Peter Macdonald (Australian politician)">Peter Macdonald</a>, the former member for the <a href="/wiki/Electoral_district_of_Manly" title="Electoral district of Manly">state seat of Manly</a>, held Abbott to only 55 percent.
</p><p>Abbott was the parliamentary secretary to the Minister for Employment, Education, Training and Youth Affairs (1996–1998), Minister for Employment Services (1998–2001), <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Employment_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Employment (Australia)">Minister for Employment and Workplace Relations and Small Business</a> (2001), Minister for Employment and Workplace Relations (2001–03) and Minister for Health and Ageing from 2003 to November 2007. From early 2002 to October 2007, he was also the <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_(Australia)" title="Leader of the House (Australia)">Leader of the House</a> in the House of Representatives.<sup id="cite_ref-ABC20011125_Insiders_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ABC20011125_Insiders-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1998, Abbott established a trust fund called "Australians for Honest Politics Trust" to help bankroll civil court cases against the <a href="/wiki/One_Nation_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="One Nation (Australia)">One Nation Party</a> and its leader <a href="/wiki/Pauline_Hanson" title="Pauline Hanson">Pauline Hanson</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Prime Minister John Howard denied any knowledge of existence of such a fund.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> Abbott was also accused of offering funds to One Nation dissident Terry Sharples to support his court battle against the party. However, Howard defended the honesty of Abbott in this matter.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Abbott conceded that the political threat One Nation posed to the Howard government was "a very big factor" in his decision to pursue the legal attack, but he also claimed to be acting "in Australia's national interest". Howard also defended Abbott's actions saying "It's the job of the Liberal Party to politically attack other parties – there's nothing wrong with that."<sup id="cite_ref-SmhAbbottSlushFund_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SmhAbbottSlushFund-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As a Parliamentary Secretary, Abbott oversaw the establishment of the <i>Green Corps</i> program which involved young people in environmental restoration work.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> As Minister for Employment Services, he oversaw the implementation of the <a href="/wiki/Job_Services_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Job Services Australia">Job Network</a> and was responsible for the government's <a href="/wiki/Work_for_the_Dole" title="Work for the Dole">Work for the Dole</a> scheme.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2000_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2000-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2002_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2002-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> He also commissioned the <a href="/wiki/Cole_Royal_Commission" class="mw-redirect" title="Cole Royal Commission">Cole Royal Commission</a> into "thuggery and rorts" in the construction industry and created the Office of the Australian Building and Construction Commissioner in response and to lift productivity.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Liberal Party allowed members a free choice in the <a href="/wiki/1999_Australian_republic_referendum" title="1999 Australian republic referendum">1999 republic referendum</a>. Abbott was one of the leading voices within the party campaigning for the successful "No" vote, pitting him against future parliamentary colleague and leading republican <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Turnbull" title="Malcolm Turnbull">Malcolm Turnbull</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Cabinet_minister_.281998.E2.80.932007.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Cabinet_minister_(1998–2007)">Cabinet minister (1998–2007)</span></h4>
<p>When Abbott was promoted to the Cabinet in 1998, Prime Minister Howard described him as an effective performer with an endearing style, whereas the Opposition described him as a "bomb thrower."<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2000_67-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2000-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> Howard appointed Abbott to replace <a href="/wiki/Kay_Patterson" title="Kay Patterson">Kay Patterson</a> as <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Health_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Health (Australia)">Minister for Health</a> in 2003, during a period of contentious <a href="/wiki/Medicare_(Australia)" title="Medicare (Australia)">Medicare</a> reform and a crisis in <a href="/wiki/Medical_indemnity" class="mw-redirect" title="Medical indemnity">Medical indemnity</a> Insurance, in which the price of insurance was forcing doctors out of practice.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Australian_Medical_Association" title="Australian Medical Association">Australian Medical Association</a> was threatening to pull out all Australian doctors.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated4_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated4-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> Abbott worked with the states to address the crisis and keep the system running.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_70-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Health care initiatives instigated by Abbott include the Nurse Family Partnership, a long term scheme aimed at improving conditions for <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_Australians" title="Indigenous Australians">indigenous</a> youth by improving mother-child relationships. The scheme was successful in reducing child abuse and improving school retention rates.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated4_74-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated4-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2005, Abbott was holidaying with his family in Bali when the <a href="/wiki/2005_Bali_bombings" title="2005 Bali bombings">Bali bombings</a> occurred. Abbott visited the victims of the bombings in hospital, and in his capacity as Health Minister organised for Australians who required lifesaving emergency surgery and hospitalisation to be flown to Singapore.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2006, Abbott controversially opposed access to the abortion drug <a href="/wiki/RU486" class="mw-redirect" title="RU486">RU486</a>, and the Parliament voted to strip Health Ministers of the power to regulate this area of policy.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> During this time, Abbott likened the act of having an abortion to committing a murder, saying "we have a bizarre double standard, a bizarre double standard in this country where someone who kills a pregnant woman's baby is guilty of murder but a woman who aborts an unborn baby is simply exercising choice".<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Abbott introduced the <a href="/wiki/Medicare_(Australia)#General_safety_net" title="Medicare (Australia)">Medicare Safety Net</a> to cap the annual <a href="/wiki/Out-of-pocket_expenses" class="mw-redirect" title="Out-of-pocket expenses">out-of-pocket costs</a> of Medicare cardholders to a maximum amount. In 2007, he attracted criticism over long delays in funding for cancer diagnostic equipment (<a href="/wiki/Positron_emission_tomography" title="Positron emission tomography">PET</a> scanners).<sup id="cite_ref-ABC20070709_730Report_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ABC20070709_730Report-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ABC20070711_News_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ABC20070711_News-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ABC20070926_730Report_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ABC20070926_730Report-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ABC20070927_News_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ABC20070927_News-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to <i><a href="/wiki/The_Sydney_Morning_Herald" title="The Sydney Morning Herald">Sydney Morning Herald</a>'s</i> political editor, <a href="/wiki/Peter_Hartcher" title="Peter Hartcher">Peter Hartcher</a>, before the defeat of the <a href="/wiki/Howard_government" title="Howard government">Howard government</a> at the <a href="/wiki/2007_Australian_federal_election" title="2007 Australian federal election">2007 election</a>, Abbott had opposed the government's centrepiece <a href="/wiki/WorkChoices" title="WorkChoices">WorkChoices</a> industrial relations deregulation reform in Cabinet, on the basis that the legislation exceeded the government's mandate, was harsh on workers, and was politically dangerous to the government.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_70-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> John Howard wrote in his 2010 autobiography that Abbott was "never a zealot about pursuing industrial relations changes" and expressed "concern about making too many changes" during Cabinet's discussion of WorkChoices.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Abbott campaigned as Minister for Health at the 2007 election. On 31 October, he apologised for saying "just because a person is sick doesn't mean that he is necessarily pure of heart in all things", after <a href="/wiki/Bernie_Banton" title="Bernie Banton">Bernie Banton</a>, an <a href="/wiki/Asbestos" title="Asbestos">asbestos</a> campaigner and terminal <a href="/wiki/Mesothelioma" title="Mesothelioma">mesothelioma</a> sufferer, complained that Abbott was unavailable to collect a petition.<sup id="cite_ref-Asbestos_Comments_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Asbestos_Comments-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> In <i>The Australian Doctor</i>'s 2015 poll, Tony Abbott was ranked as the third "worst health minister in 35 years", as voted on by doctors. <a href="/wiki/Peter_Dutton" title="Peter Dutton">Peter Dutton</a> was ranked the overall worst.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Shadow_minister_.282007.E2.80.932009.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Shadow_minister_(2007–2009)">Shadow minister (2007–2009)</span></h4>
<p>The Coalition lost government in 2007 and Abbott was re-elected to the seat of Warringah with a 1.8% swing toward the Labor Party.<sup id="cite_ref-2007_Election_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2007_Election-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> Following <a href="/wiki/Peter_Costello" title="Peter Costello">Peter Costello</a>'s rejection of the leadership of the Parliamentary Liberal Party, Abbott <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill,_2007" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill, 2007">nominated for the position of party leader</a>, along with Malcolm Turnbull and <a href="/wiki/Brendan_Nelson" title="Brendan Nelson">Brendan Nelson</a>. After canvassing the support of his colleagues, Abbott decided to withdraw his nomination. He seemingly did not have the numbers, noting that he was "obviously very closely identified with the outgoing prime minister."<sup id="cite_ref-Liberal_leadership_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Liberal_leadership-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> He said he would not rule out contesting the leadership at some time in the future.<sup id="cite_ref-May_run_again_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-May_run_again-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> Of the three candidates, Abbott was the only one who had previous experience in Opposition. Nelson was elected Liberal leader in December 2007 and Abbott was assigned the Shadow Portfolio of Families, Community Services and Indigenous Affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-Brendan_Nelson_assigns_shadow_ministry_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brendan_Nelson_assigns_shadow_ministry-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> As indigenous affairs spokesman, Abbott said that it had been a mistake for the Howard government not to offer a <a href="/wiki/Stolen_Generations" title="Stolen Generations">national apology to the Stolen Generations</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated5_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated5-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> spent time teaching at remote Aboriginal communities;<sup id="cite_ref-eurekastreet1_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-eurekastreet1-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> and argued for the Rudd government to continue the <a href="/wiki/Northern_Territory_National_Emergency_Response" title="Northern Territory National Emergency Response">Northern Territory National Emergency Response</a> which restricted alcohol and introduced conditional welfare in certain Aboriginal communities.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated6_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated6-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During this period in Opposition, Abbott wrote <i><a href="/wiki/Battlelines" title="Battlelines">Battlelines</a></i>, a biography and reflection on the Howard government, and potential future policy direction for the Liberal Party.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> In the book, Abbott said that in certain aspects the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Federation" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Federation">Australian Federation</a> was "dysfunctional" and in need of repair. He recommended the establishment of local hospital and school boards to manage health and education,<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> and discussed family law reform, multiculturalism, climate change, and international relations. The book received a favourable review from former Labor Party speech writer <a href="/wiki/Bob_Ellis" title="Bob Ellis">Bob Ellis</a> and <i>The Australian</i> described it as "read almost universally as Abbott's intellectual application for the party's leadership after the Turnbull experiment".<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The number of unauthorised immigrant arrivals in boats to Australia increased during 2008.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> Abbott claimed that this was an effect of the Rudd government's easing of border protection laws and accused <a href="/wiki/Kevin_Rudd" title="Kevin Rudd">Kevin Rudd</a> of ineptitude and hypocrisy on the issue of unauthorised immigrants upon boats arriving, particularly during the <a href="/wiki/MV_Oceanic_Viking" title="MV Oceanic Viking">Oceanic Viking affair of October 2009</a>, saying, "John Howard found a problem and created a solution. Kevin Rudd found a solution and has now created a problem".<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During November 2009, Abbott resigned from shadow ministerial responsibilities due to the Liberal Party's position on the Rudd government's <a href="/wiki/Emissions_trading" title="Emissions trading">Emissions trading</a> Scheme (ETS), leading to the resignation of other shadow ministers.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Leader_of_the_Opposition_.282009.E2.80.932013.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Leader_of_the_Opposition_(2009–2013)">Leader of the Opposition (2009–2013)</span></h2>
<p>On 1 December 2009, Abbott was <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill,_2009" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill, 2009">elected to the position of Leader of the Liberal Party of Australia</a> over Turnbull and Shadow Treasurer <a href="/wiki/Joe_Hockey" title="Joe Hockey">Joe Hockey</a>. Abbott proposed blocking the government's ETS in the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Senate" title="Australian Senate">Senate</a> whereas Turnbull sought to amend the bill which the majority of the Liberal Party did not support.<sup id="cite_ref-Leadership_ballot_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Leadership_ballot-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> Abbott named his <a href="/wiki/Shadow_Cabinet_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Shadow Cabinet of Australia">Shadow Cabinet</a> on 8 December 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Abbott described Prime Minister Rudd's Emission Trading plan as a 'Great big tax on everything' and opposed it. The Coalition and minor parties voted against the government's ETS legislation in the Senate and the legislation was rejected. Abbott announced a new Coalition policy on carbon emission reduction in February, which committed the Coalition to a 5 per cent reduction in emissions by 2020. Abbott proposed the creation of an 'emissions reduction fund' to provide 'direct' incentives to industry and farmers to reduce carbon emissions. In April, Rudd announced that plans for the introduction his ETS would be delayed until 2013.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When appointed to the Liberal leadership, Abbott's Catholicism and moral beliefs became subjects of repeated media questioning. Various commentators suggested that his traditionalist views would polarise female voters.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> He told press gallery journalist <a href="/wiki/Laurie_Oakes" title="Laurie Oakes">Laurie Oakes</a> that he did not do doorstop interviews in front of church but regularly faced pointed questions about his faith which were not being put to Prime Minister Rudd, who conducted weekly church door press conferences following his attendances at Anglican services.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Abbott reportedly missed the 2009 vote on the Rudd government $42&#160;billion <a href="/wiki/National_fiscal_policy_response_to_the_Great_Recession#Australia" title="National fiscal policy response to the Great Recession">stimulus package</a> because he fell asleep in his parliamentary office after a night of drinking. When asked by a journalist whether he had been drunk, Abbott said "that is an impertinent question" and that he "wasn't keeping count" but thought it was "maybe two" bottles of wine.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In a <i><a href="/wiki/60_Minutes_(Australian_TV_program)" title="60 Minutes (Australian TV program)">60 Minutes</a></i> interview aired on 7 March 2010, Abbott was asked: "Homosexuality? How do you feel about that?". He replied: "I'd probably feel a bit threatened … it's a fact of life and I try to treat people as people and not put them in pigeonholes."<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> In later interviews Abbott apologised for the remark.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-abc.net.au_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-abc.net.au-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> In 2013, Abbott stated on 3AW that if his sister <a href="/wiki/Christine_Forster" title="Christine Forster">Christine Forster</a> were to have a marriage ceremony with her partner Virginia he would attend.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Rear_Admiral_Davyd_Thomas_AM,_CSC,_RAN,_Major_General_Paul_Symon,_AO_and_Air_Marshal_Mark_Binskin,_AM_during_the_wreath_laying_at_the_NADC.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/93/Rear_Admiral_Davyd_Thomas_AM%2C_CSC%2C_RAN%2C_Major_General_Paul_Symon%2C_AO_and_Air_Marshal_Mark_Binskin%2C_AM_during_the_wreath_laying_at_the_NADC.jpg/230px-Rear_Admiral_Davyd_Thomas_AM%2C_CSC%2C_RAN%2C_Major_General_Paul_Symon%2C_AO_and_Air_Marshal_Mark_Binskin%2C_AM_during_the_wreath_laying_at_the_NADC.jpg" decoding="async" width="230" height="154" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/93/Rear_Admiral_Davyd_Thomas_AM%2C_CSC%2C_RAN%2C_Major_General_Paul_Symon%2C_AO_and_Air_Marshal_Mark_Binskin%2C_AM_during_the_wreath_laying_at_the_NADC.jpg/345px-Rear_Admiral_Davyd_Thomas_AM%2C_CSC%2C_RAN%2C_Major_General_Paul_Symon%2C_AO_and_Air_Marshal_Mark_Binskin%2C_AM_during_the_wreath_laying_at_the_NADC.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/93/Rear_Admiral_Davyd_Thomas_AM%2C_CSC%2C_RAN%2C_Major_General_Paul_Symon%2C_AO_and_Air_Marshal_Mark_Binskin%2C_AM_during_the_wreath_laying_at_the_NADC.jpg/460px-Rear_Admiral_Davyd_Thomas_AM%2C_CSC%2C_RAN%2C_Major_General_Paul_Symon%2C_AO_and_Air_Marshal_Mark_Binskin%2C_AM_during_the_wreath_laying_at_the_NADC.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="1335" /></a><figcaption>Attending the 2010 <a href="/wiki/Anzac_Day" title="Anzac Day">Anzac Day</a> National Service at the <a href="/wiki/Australian_War_Memorial" title="Australian War Memorial">Australian War Memorial</a> in Canberra</figcaption></figure>
<p>In March 2010, Abbott, announced a new policy initiative to provide for six months paid <a href="/wiki/Parental_leave" title="Parental leave">parental leave</a>, funded by an increase in corporate tax by 1.7 percentage points on all taxable company income above $5&#160;million. Business groups and the government opposed the plan, however it won support from the Australian Greens.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While Opposition Spokesman for Indigenous Affairs, Abbott spent time in remote <a href="/wiki/Cape_York_Peninsula" title="Cape York Peninsula">Cape York</a> Aboriginal communities as a teacher, organised through prominent indigenous activist <a href="/wiki/Noel_Pearson" title="Noel Pearson">Noel Pearson</a>. Abbott repeatedly spoke of his admiration for Pearson, and in March 2010, introduced the <i>Wild Rivers (Environmental Management) Bill</i> to Parliament in support of Pearson's campaign to overturn the Queensland government's <i>Wild Rivers</i> legislation. Abbott and Pearson believed that the Queensland law would 'block the economic development' of indigenous land, and interfere with <a href="/wiki/Aboriginal_land_rights" class="mw-redirect" title="Aboriginal land rights">Aboriginal land rights</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Abbott completed an <a href="/wiki/Ironman_Triathlon" title="Ironman Triathlon">Ironman Triathlon</a> event in March 2010 at <a href="/wiki/Port_Macquarie" title="Port Macquarie">Port Macquarie</a>, New South Wales. In April he set out on a 9-day charity bike ride between Melbourne and Sydney, the annual Pollie Pedal, generating political debate about whether he should have committed so much time to physical fitness.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> Abbott described the events as an opportunity to "stop at lots of little towns along the way where people probably never see or don't very often see a federal member of Parliament."<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his first Budget reply speech as Opposition Leader, Abbott sought to portray the Rudd government's third budget as a "tax and spend" budget and promised to fight the election on the new mining "super-profits" tax proposed by <a href="/wiki/Kevin_Rudd" title="Kevin Rudd">Rudd</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2010_election">2010 election</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2010_Australian_federal_election" title="2010 Australian federal election">2010 Australian federal election</a></div>
<p>On 24 June 2010, <a href="/wiki/Julia_Gillard" title="Julia Gillard">Julia Gillard</a> replaced Kevin Rudd as Australian Labor Party leader and Prime Minister.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> The replacement of a first-term Prime Minister was unusual in Australian political history and the Rudd-Gillard rivalry remained a vexed issue for the <a href="/wiki/Gillard_government" title="Gillard government">Gillard government</a> into the 2010 election and its subsequent term. On 17 July, Gillard called the 2010 federal election for 21 August.<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> Polls in the first week gave a view that Labor would be re-elected with an increased majority, with <a href="/wiki/Newspoll" title="Newspoll">Newspoll</a> and an <a href="/wiki/Essential_poll" class="mw-redirect" title="Essential poll">Essential poll</a> showing a lead of 10 points (55–45) <a href="/wiki/Two-party-preferred_vote" title="Two-party-preferred vote">two party preferred</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-archer1_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-archer1-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The two leaders met for one official debate during the campaign. Studio audience surveys by <a href="/wiki/Nine_Network" title="Nine Network">Channel 9</a> and <a href="/wiki/Seven_Network" title="Seven Network">Seven Network</a> suggested a win to Gillard.<sup id="cite_ref-archer1_123-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-archer1-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> Unable to agree on further debates, the leaders went on to appear separately on stage for questioning at community fora in Sydney and Brisbane. In Sydney on 11 August, Abbott's opening statement focused on his main election messages around government debt, taxation and asylum seekers. An exit poll of the Rooty Hill RSL audience accorded Abbott victory.<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> Gillard won the audience poll at Broncos Leagues Club meeting in Brisbane on 18 August.<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> Abbott appeared for public questioning on the ABC's <i>Q&amp;A</i> program on 16 August.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Labor and the Coalition each won 72 seats in the 150-seat <a href="/wiki/Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Australian House of Representatives">House of Representatives</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-72all_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72all-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> four short of the requirement for <a href="/wiki/Majority_government" title="Majority government">majority government</a>, resulting in the first <a href="/wiki/Hung_parliament" title="Hung parliament">hung parliament</a> since the <a href="/wiki/1940_Australian_federal_election" title="1940 Australian federal election">1940 election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-hung_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hung-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-bbc_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bbc-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Abbott and Gillard commenced a 17-day period of negotiation with <a href="/wiki/Crossbencher" title="Crossbencher">crossbenchers</a> over who would form government. On the crossbench, four <a href="/wiki/Independent_(politics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Independent (politics)">independent</a> members, one member of the <a href="/wiki/National_Party_of_Western_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="National Party of Western Australia">National Party of Western Australia</a> and one member of the Australian Greens held the <a href="/wiki/Balance_of_power_(parliament)" title="Balance of power (parliament)">balance of power</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> Following the negotiations, Gillard formed a <a href="/wiki/Minority_government" title="Minority government">minority government</a> with the support of an Australian Greens MP and three independent MPs on the basis of <a href="/wiki/Confidence_and_supply" title="Confidence and supply">confidence and supply</a>. Another independent and the WA National gave their confidence and supply support to the Coalition, resulting in Labor holding a 76–74 tally of votes on the floor of the Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> The Coalition finished with 49.88 percent of the <a href="/wiki/Two-party-preferred_vote" title="Two-party-preferred vote">two party preferred vote</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> obtaining a national swing of around 2.6%.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During negotiations, the Independents requested that both major parties' policies be costed by the apolitical Australian Treasury. The Coalition initially resisted the idea, citing concerns over Treasury leaks, however they eventually allowed the analysis. Treasury endorsed Labor's budget costings but projected that Coalition policies would add between $860&#160;million and $4.5&#160;billion to the bottom line over the next four years, rather than the $11.5&#160;billion
projected by the Coalition.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> The close result was lauded by former Prime Minister John Howard, who wrote in 2010 that Abbott had shifted the dynamic of Australian politics after coming to the leadership in 2009 and "deserves hero status among Liberals".<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="After_the_2010_election">After the 2010 election</span></h3>
<p>Following the 2010 election, Abbott and his deputy, <a href="/wiki/Julie_Bishop" title="Julie Bishop">Julie Bishop</a>, were re-elected unopposed as leaders of the Liberal Party.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> Abbott announced his shadow ministry on 14 September, with few changes to senior positions, but with the return of former leadership rival Malcolm Turnbull, whom he selected as Communications spokesman.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> Abbott announced that he wanted Turnbull to prosecute the Opposition's case against the <a href="/wiki/Gillard_government" title="Gillard government">Gillard government</a>'s proposed expenditure on a National Broadband Network.<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the <a href="/wiki/2010%E2%80%932011_Queensland_floods" title="2010–2011 Queensland floods">2010–2011 Queensland floods</a>, Abbott opposed plans by the Gillard government to impose a "flood levy" on taxpayers to fund reconstruction efforts. Abbott said that funding should be found within the existing budget.<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> Abbott announced a proposal for a taskforce to examine further construction of dams in Australia to deal with flood impact and food security.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In February 2011, Abbott criticised the Gillard government's handling of health reform and proposal for a 50–50 public hospitals funding arrangement with the states and territories, describing the revised Labor Party proposal as "the biggest surrender since Singapore".<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> Although Abbott had previously stated that he considered a carbon tax the best way to set a <a href="/wiki/Carbon_price" title="Carbon price">price on carbon</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> he opposed Prime Minister Gillard's February 2011 announcement of a proposal for the introduction of a "carbon tax", and called on her to take the issue to an election. Abbott said that Gillard had lied to the electorate over the issue because Gillard and her Treasurer <a href="/wiki/Wayne_Swan" title="Wayne Swan">Wayne Swan</a> had ruled out the introduction of a carbon tax in the lead up to the 2010 election.<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In April 2011, Abbott proposed consultation with <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_Australians" title="Indigenous Australians">Indigenous people</a> over a bipartisan Federal Government intervention in <a href="/wiki/Northern_Territory" title="Northern Territory">Northern Territory</a> towns including Alice Springs, Katherine and Tennant Creek, which would cover such areas as police numbers and school attendance in an effort to address what he described as a "failed state" situation.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> April saw Abbott announce a $430&#160;million policy plan to improve the employment prospects of people with serious mental health problems.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the first Gillard government budget in May 2011, Abbott used his budget-reply speech to reiterate his critiques of government policy and call for an early election over the issue of a carbon tax.<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> Rhetorically echoing Liberal party founder, <a href="/wiki/Robert_Menzies" title="Robert Menzies">Robert Menzies</a>, Abbott addressed remarks to the "forgotten families".<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In June 2011, Abbott for the first time led Gillard in a Newspoll as preferred Prime Minister.<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> In September 2011, he announced a plan to develop an agricultural food bowl in the north of Australia by developing dams for irrigation and hydroelectricity. Coalition task force leader Andrew Robb claimed that Australia currently produced enough food for 60 million people, but that the Coalition plan could double this to 120&#160;million people by 2040.<sup id="cite_ref-foodbowl_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-foodbowl-153">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> The head of the Northern Australia Land and Water Taskforce expressed concerns about the economic and environmental viability of this plan as well as its effects on the indigenous Australian communities in northern Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Reflecting on indigenous issues on the occasion of the 40th anniversary of the <a href="/wiki/Aboriginal_Tent_Embassy" title="Aboriginal Tent Embassy">Aboriginal Tent Embassy</a> on Australia Day 2012, Abbott said that there had been many positive developments in indigenous affairs in recent decades including Rudd's apology and moves to include indigenous Australians in the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Constitution">Australian Constitution</a>. Later that day, Abbott became the target of protesters from the "Embassy" after one of Gillard's advisers contacted a union official who advised Tent Embassy protesters of Abbott's whereabouts and misrepresented Abbott's views on Aboriginal affairs to them, saying he intended to "pull down" the embassy. A major security scare resulted, which was broadcast around the world, resulting in Gillard and Abbott being rushed to a government car amid a throng of security due to fears for their safety.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:2012_Lake_to_Lagoon_fun_run_begins.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/2012_Lake_to_Lagoon_fun_run_begins.jpg/220px-2012_Lake_to_Lagoon_fun_run_begins.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/2012_Lake_to_Lagoon_fun_run_begins.jpg/330px-2012_Lake_to_Lagoon_fun_run_begins.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/2012_Lake_to_Lagoon_fun_run_begins.jpg/440px-2012_Lake_to_Lagoon_fun_run_begins.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="1333" /></a><figcaption>Lake to Lagoon competitors at the starting line, including Tony Abbott, <a href="/wiki/Wagga_Wagga" title="Wagga Wagga">Wagga Wagga</a>, 2012</figcaption></figure>
<p>In an address to the <a href="/wiki/National_Press_Club_(Australia)" title="National Press Club (Australia)">National Press Club</a> on 31 January 2012, Abbott outlined some of his plans for government if elected. These included an intent to live one week of every year in an indigenous Australian community, and to prune government expenditure and cut taxes. Abbott also announced "aspirational" targets for a disability insurance scheme and a subsidised dentistry program once the budget had been restored to "strong surplus".<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Abbott responded to the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party_leadership_spill,_2012" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Labor Party leadership spill, 2012">February 2012 Labor leadership crisis</a> by criticising the cross bench independents for keeping Labor in power and renewed his calls for a general election to select the next Prime Minister of Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In criticising the Gillard government on foreign policy, Abbott said that "foreign policy should have a Jakarta rather than a Geneva focus".<sup id="cite_ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> Following his attendance at the 10th anniversary commemoration of the Bali bombing in Bali, Abbott travelled to Jakarta with his Shadow Ministers for Foreign Affairs and Immigration for a meeting with Indonesian President Yudhoyono and Foreign Minister <a href="/wiki/Marty_Natalegawa" title="Marty Natalegawa">Marty Natalegawa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> Abbott promised a "no-surprises principle" for dealings with Indonesia. The presidential reception was an unusual occurrence for an opposition leader.<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In November 2012, Abbott launched his fourth book, <i>A Strong Australia</i>, a compilation of nine of his "landmark speeches" from 2012, including his budget reply and National Press Club addresses.<sup id="cite_ref-Ireland,_Judith_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ireland,_Judith-161">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tony_Abbott_Dec_2012.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fc/Tony_Abbott_Dec_2012.jpg/170px-Tony_Abbott_Dec_2012.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="231" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fc/Tony_Abbott_Dec_2012.jpg/255px-Tony_Abbott_Dec_2012.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fc/Tony_Abbott_Dec_2012.jpg/340px-Tony_Abbott_Dec_2012.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1439" data-file-height="1958" /></a><figcaption>Abbott as Leader of the Opposition</figcaption></figure>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Gillard_misogyny_speech">Gillard misogyny speech</span></h4>
<p>On 9 October 2012, Prime Minister Julia Gillard accused Tony Abbott of misogyny and hypocrisy in a <a href="/wiki/Misogyny_Speech" title="Misogyny Speech">speech to Parliament that gained international notice</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-162">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister_.282013.E2.80.932015.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister_(2013–2015)">Prime Minister (2013–2015)</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Abbott_government" title="Abbott government">Abbott government</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_policy_implementation">Early policy implementation</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tony_Abbott_being_sworn_in_by_Quentin_Bryce_(1).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/Tony_Abbott_being_sworn_in_by_Quentin_Bryce_%281%29.jpg/220px-Tony_Abbott_being_sworn_in_by_Quentin_Bryce_%281%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/Tony_Abbott_being_sworn_in_by_Quentin_Bryce_%281%29.jpg/330px-Tony_Abbott_being_sworn_in_by_Quentin_Bryce_%281%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/Tony_Abbott_being_sworn_in_by_Quentin_Bryce_%281%29.jpg/440px-Tony_Abbott_being_sworn_in_by_Quentin_Bryce_%281%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="426" /></a><figcaption>Tony Abbott being sworn in as Prime Minister by Quentin Bryce, 18 September 2013</figcaption></figure>
<p>At the <a href="/wiki/2013_Australian_federal_election" title="2013 Australian federal election">federal election</a> on 7 September 2013, Abbott led the <a href="/wiki/Coalition_(Australia)" title="Coalition (Australia)">Liberal-National coalition</a> to victory over the incumbent Labor government, led by Kevin Rudd. Abbott and <a href="/wiki/Abbott_Ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="Abbott Ministry">his ministry</a> were sworn in on 18 September 2013.<sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> He was the subject of criticism for his decision to only include one woman, Deputy Liberal leader Julie Bishop, in his cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On the first day of the new Parliament, Abbott introduced legislation into Parliament to repeal the Carbon Tax, and commenced <a href="/wiki/Operation_Sovereign_Borders" title="Operation Sovereign Borders">Operation Sovereign Borders</a>, the Coalition's policy to stop the maritime arrival of asylum seekers, which received strong public support.<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Abbott announced a <a href="/wiki/Royal_Commission_into_trade_union_governance_and_corruption" class="mw-redirect" title="Royal Commission into trade union governance and corruption">Royal Commission into trade union governance and corruption</a> on 11 February 2014.<sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> This was followed by amendments to the <a href="/wiki/Fair_Work_Commission" title="Fair Work Commission">Fair Work Act</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> and a "Repeal Day", where more than 10,000 "red tape" regulations were repealed.<sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As Prime Minister, Abbott oversaw free trade agreements signed with Japan, South Korea and China.<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Carbon Tax Repeal Bill passed both houses of Parliament on 17 July 2014 and the Mining Tax Repeal Bill passed both houses of Parliament on 2 September 2014 after negotiations with the <a href="/wiki/Palmer_United_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="Palmer United Party">Palmer United Party</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
