<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Abbott_and_Robb_signing_the_Free_Trade_Agreement_with_Chinese_President_Xi_and_Minister_for_Commerce_Gao_Hucheng_November_2014.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/Abbott_and_Robb_signing_the_Free_Trade_Agreement_with_Chinese_President_Xi_and_Minister_for_Commerce_Gao_Hucheng_November_2014.jpg/220px-Abbott_and_Robb_signing_the_Free_Trade_Agreement_with_Chinese_President_Xi_and_Minister_for_Commerce_Gao_Hucheng_November_2014.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/Abbott_and_Robb_signing_the_Free_Trade_Agreement_with_Chinese_President_Xi_and_Minister_for_Commerce_Gao_Hucheng_November_2014.jpg/330px-Abbott_and_Robb_signing_the_Free_Trade_Agreement_with_Chinese_President_Xi_and_Minister_for_Commerce_Gao_Hucheng_November_2014.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/Abbott_and_Robb_signing_the_Free_Trade_Agreement_with_Chinese_President_Xi_and_Minister_for_Commerce_Gao_Hucheng_November_2014.jpg/440px-Abbott_and_Robb_signing_the_Free_Trade_Agreement_with_Chinese_President_Xi_and_Minister_for_Commerce_Gao_Hucheng_November_2014.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5287" data-file-height="3525" /></a><figcaption>Abbott and Andrew Robb signing the <a href="/wiki/China%E2%80%93Australia_Free_Trade_Agreement" title="China–Australia Free Trade Agreement">Free Trade Agreement</a> with president and <a href="/wiki/Chinese_Communist_Party" title="Chinese Communist Party">Chinese Communist Party</a> leader <a href="/wiki/Xi_Jinping" title="Xi Jinping">Xi Jinping</a>, November 2014</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Secretary_Kerry_Greets_Australia%27s_Prime_Minister_Abbott_(10152933465).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Secretary_Kerry_Greets_Australia%27s_Prime_Minister_Abbott_%2810152933465%29.jpg/220px-Secretary_Kerry_Greets_Australia%27s_Prime_Minister_Abbott_%2810152933465%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Secretary_Kerry_Greets_Australia%27s_Prime_Minister_Abbott_%2810152933465%29.jpg/330px-Secretary_Kerry_Greets_Australia%27s_Prime_Minister_Abbott_%2810152933465%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Secretary_Kerry_Greets_Australia%27s_Prime_Minister_Abbott_%2810152933465%29.jpg/440px-Secretary_Kerry_Greets_Australia%27s_Prime_Minister_Abbott_%2810152933465%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2844" data-file-height="1893" /></a><figcaption>Abbott meeting with US Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/John_Kerry" title="John Kerry">John Kerry</a></figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2014_budget">2014 budget</span></h3>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/2014_Australian_federal_budget" title="2014 Australian federal budget">2014 Australian federal budget</a>, the Abbott government's first budget, delivered by Treasurer Joe Hockey, was criticised by the Opposition as "cruel" and "unfair" and a large number of budget saving measures were blocked by the crossbench in the Senate. Hockey and Abbott were both criticised for their inability to "sell" the necessity of the budget cuts to the cross bench or the public. Hockey was further criticised for several "out of touch" and "insensitive" comments in subsequent months, however, the prime minister continuously publicly backed the treasurer, refusing to replace him with a better performing minister.<sup id="cite_ref-near_177-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-near-177">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Knighting_of_Prince_Philip">Knighting of Prince Philip</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tony_Abbott_September_2014.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Tony_Abbott_September_2014.jpg/170px-Tony_Abbott_September_2014.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="238" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Tony_Abbott_September_2014.jpg/255px-Tony_Abbott_September_2014.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Tony_Abbott_September_2014.jpg/340px-Tony_Abbott_September_2014.jpg 2x" data-file-width="439" data-file-height="614" /></a><figcaption>Abbott in 2014</figcaption></figure>
<p>On 25 March 2014, Abbott announced that he had advised the Queen to reinstate the knight and dame system of honours to the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Australia" title="Order of Australia">Order of Australia</a>. Outgoing Governor-General <a href="/wiki/Quentin_Bryce" title="Quentin Bryce">Quentin Bryce</a> and her successor, <a href="/wiki/Peter_Cosgrove" title="Peter Cosgrove">Peter Cosgrove</a>, became the first recipients of the reinstated honours. On Australia Day 2015, Abbott announced that <a href="/wiki/Prince_Philip,_Duke_of_Edinburgh" title="Prince Philip, Duke of Edinburgh">Prince Philip, Duke of Edinburgh</a>, the Queen's husband and a resident of the United Kingdom, would be appointed a Knight of the Order of Australia. This decision was widely criticised, including by members of the government, and fuelled speculation that the prime minister's leadership could be challenged.<sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup> On 2 November 2015, new prime minister Malcolm Turnbull announced that knights and dames had been removed from the Order of Australia, as "not appropriate in our modern honours system", although existing titles would not be affected.<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="February_2015_leadership_spill">February 2015 leadership spill</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tony_Abbott_January_2015_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/91/Tony_Abbott_January_2015_%28cropped%29.jpg/170px-Tony_Abbott_January_2015_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="223" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/91/Tony_Abbott_January_2015_%28cropped%29.jpg/255px-Tony_Abbott_January_2015_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/91/Tony_Abbott_January_2015_%28cropped%29.jpg/340px-Tony_Abbott_January_2015_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="720" data-file-height="945" /></a><figcaption>Abbott speaking at the 2015 National Flag Raising and Citizenship Ceremony</figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/February_2015_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill_motion" title="February 2015 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill motion">February 2015 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill motion</a></div>
<p>On 6 February 2015, Liberal backbencher <a href="/wiki/Luke_Simpkins" title="Luke Simpkins">Luke Simpkins</a> announced that he would move a motion, at a meeting of the party room, for a spill of the federal Liberal Party's leadership positions. Simpkins stated that such a motion would give Liberal members of parliament and senators the opportunity to either endorse the Prime Minister or "seek a new direction."<sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> The meeting was held on 9 February 2015 and the <a href="/wiki/February_2015_Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill_motion" title="February 2015 Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill motion">spill motion</a> was defeated by 61 votes to 39.<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> Both Malcolm Turnbull and deputy leader Julie Bishop were speculated to be considering a leadership run if the spill motion had succeeded.<sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> Prime Minister Abbott described the leadership motion as a "near death experience" and declared that "good government starts today", promising to consult his colleagues more, to shy away from his so-called "captain's calls" and to reduce the role of his chief of staff <a href="/wiki/Peta_Credlin" title="Peta Credlin">Peta Credlin</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-near_177-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-near-177">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Choppergate">Choppergate</span></h3>
<p>In July 2015, <a href="/wiki/Bronwyn_Bishop" title="Bronwyn Bishop">Bronwyn Bishop</a>, who had been successfully nominated by Abbott in November 2013 for the position of <a href="/wiki/Speaker_of_the_Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Speaker of the Australian House of Representatives">Speaker of the House</a>, came under intense media scrutiny after details of her use of taxpayer-funded political entitlements were made public, including chartering a helicopter flight between Melbourne and Geelong to attend a Liberal party fundraiser. Abbott was criticised over his handling of the entitlements scandal as he allowed the controversy to drag on for weeks because of his refusal to sack the Speaker, a close friend and political mentor. Despite Abbott's support, Bishop resigned as Speaker on 2 August 2015.<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Same-sex_marriage_debate">Same-sex marriage debate</span></h3>
<p>During Abbott's prime ministership, Australian law continued to define marriage as a union of a man and a woman, while recognising same-sex couples as de facto couples in areas such as taxation law, social security law, immigration and superannuation, and Abbott did not support changing the law.<sup id="cite_ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> During Abbott's time as Opposition Leader and Prime Minister, the position of the Labor Party and opinion polls shifted towards favouring same-sex marriage. Abbott determined that a national plebiscite, rather than a Parliamentary vote should settle the issue.<sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As an Opposition front bencher in 2008, Abbott wrote: "The love and commitment between two people of the same sex can be as strong as that between husband and wife... There is more moral quality in a relationship between two people devoted to each other for decades than in many a short-lived marriage. Still, however deeply affectionate or long lasting it may be, the relationship between two people of the same sex cannot be a marriage because a marriage, by definition, is between a man and a woman... Let's celebrate all strong relationships, whether they are between a man and a woman or between people of the same sex but let's be careful about describing every lasting sexual bond as a 'marriage'."<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/First_Rudd_government" class="mw-redirect" title="First Rudd government">First Rudd government</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gillard_government" title="Gillard government">Gillard government</a> held similar views (although the short-lived second Rudd government reversed Labor's position on the issue).<sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Abbott reaffirmed that he did not support changing the law to recognise same-sex marriage, and did not alter Coalition policy on the issue – however he permitted Coalition members to advocate for change if they felt strongly on the issue, and indicated that if a bill were to come before the new parliament, the Coalition party room would discuss its stance on the issue.<sup id="cite_ref-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-191">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> Opinion polls suggested growing support for change.<sup id="cite_ref-192" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-192">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup> On 11 August 2015, after renewed debate about <a href="/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_Australia" title="Same-sex marriage in Australia">same-sex marriage in Australia</a>, Abbott called a Coalition Party room vote and Coalition MPs voted against allowing a free vote on the issue 66 to 33.<sup id="cite_ref-193" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-193">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> Some MPs said they were willing to cross the floor on the issue and Abbott was criticised by some pro-gay marriage Liberal MPs, including Christopher Pyne, for holding the vote in the Coalition party room, rather than the Liberal party room (as the inclusion of National Party votes decreased chances of a pro-change outcome).<sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> To settle the issue, Abbott proposed a plebiscite following the next election. Although he remained personally opposed to change, he said Parliament should respect the outcome of the national vote on the issue.<sup id="cite_ref-195" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-195">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> A <a href="/wiki/Australian_Marriage_Law_Postal_Survey" title="Australian Marriage Law Postal Survey">national plebiscite</a> regarding same-sex marriage would eventually be held in 2017, under the subsequent <a href="/wiki/Turnbull_government" title="Turnbull government">Turnbull government</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-196" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-196">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="September_2015_leadership_spill">September 2015 leadership spill</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spill,_September_2015" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill, September 2015">Liberal Party of Australia leadership spill, September 2015</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tony_abbott_bust.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Tony_abbott_bust.jpg/170px-Tony_abbott_bust.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="237" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/22/Tony_abbott_bust.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="229" data-file-height="319" /></a><figcaption>Bronze bust of Tony Abbott at the <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister%27s_Avenue" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime Minister&#39;s Avenue">Prime Minister's Avenue</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Ballarat_Botanical_Gardens" title="Ballarat Botanical Gardens">Ballarat Botanical Gardens</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>On 14 September 2015, Malcolm Turnbull, the Minister for Communications, resigned and stated his intention to challenge the Liberal Party leadership in a <a href="/wiki/Leadership_spill" title="Leadership spill">leadership spill</a>. A party-room meeting held that evening saw Abbott defeated by Turnbull on a 54–44 vote.<sup id="cite_ref-197" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-197">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> According to <i><a href="/wiki/The_Economist" title="The Economist">The Economist</a></i>, Abbott was ousted due to poor opinion polling, lacklustre economic management, and involvement in several political gaffes and scandals.<sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> In comments just after the result was announced, Turnbull praised Abbott for his "formidable achievements" as prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> By the time he was removed from premiership, Abbott was one of the most unpopular world leaders,<sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> and he has been regarded by critics and political experts as one of Australia's worst prime ministers.<sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Later_years_in_parliament_.282015.E2.80.932019.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_years_in_parliament_(2015–2019)">Later years in parliament (2015–2019)</span></h2>
<p>After <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_Turnbull" title="Malcolm Turnbull">Malcolm Turnbull</a> successfully challenged Abbott for the Liberal Party leadership in 2015, Abbott returned to the government backbench and pledged to remain in Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-205">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> In a final media conference as Prime Minister, Abbott told reporters: "Leadership changes are never easy for our country. My pledge today is to make this change as easy as I can. There will be no wrecking, no undermining, and no sniping. I've never leaked or backgrounded against anyone. And I certainly won't start now [...] I am proud of what the Abbott government has achieved. We stayed focused despite the white-anting [...] The nature of politics has changed in the past decade. A febrile media culture has developed that rewards treachery. If there's one piece of advice I can give to the media, it's this: refuse to print self-serving claims that the person making them won't put his or her name to. Refuse to connive at dishonour by acting as the assassin's knife."<sup id="cite_ref-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-206">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<dl><dt>Turnbull government</dt></dl>
<p>Malcolm Turnbull gave the former Prime Minister no portfolio in the <a href="/wiki/Turnbull_government" title="Turnbull government">new government</a>. As a backbencher, he continued to defend the record of the Abbott government, and speak out on a range of issues. Following the <a href="/wiki/November_2015_Paris_attacks" title="November 2015 Paris attacks">November 2015 Paris attacks</a>, he backed Egyptian President <a href="/wiki/Abdel_Fattah_el-Sisi" title="Abdel Fattah el-Sisi">Abdel Fattah el-Sisi</a> call for a "revolution in Islam" in a speech in Singapore, and told Sky News, "All of those things that Islam has never had — a Reformation, an Enlightenment, a well-developed concept of the separation of church and state — that needs to happen."<sup id="cite_ref-abc_2015-12-09_207-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-abc_2015-12-09-207">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup> He defended the Abbott government's 2014 Budget measures and called on future Prime Ministers to follow his commitment to spending a week a year in indigenous communities.<sup id="cite_ref-abc_2015-12-09_207-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-abc_2015-12-09-207">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup> Abbott declared he would have won the 2016 Election.<sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> In a December 2015 editorial, <i><a href="/wiki/The_Sydney_Morning_Herald" title="The Sydney Morning Herald">The Sydney Morning Herald</a></i> accused Abbott of contradicting his undertaking that there would be "no undermining of Malcolm Turnbull".<sup id="cite_ref-smh9dec_209-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-smh9dec-209">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 24 January 2016, Abbott confirmed that he would stand for Liberal preselection for the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Warringah" title="Division of Warringah">Division of Warringah</a> in the federal election.<sup id="cite_ref-210" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-210">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> He was re-elected with a small swing against him, matching the statewide swing against the Government.<sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup> Following his re-election, he voiced various concerns about the direction of the Turnbull government.<sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-213" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-213">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-214" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-214">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> In February 2017, he told a book launch that the Turnbull government was perceived by many conservatives as "Labor lite", and risked a "drift to defeat" at the upcoming election if it failed to improve its performance.<sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull's ousting of Abbott had divided the Liberal Party rank and file and tensions continued in the parliamentary Party.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_216-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-216">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto1_217-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto1-217">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto2_218-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto2-218">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup> Abbott said Turnbull supporters had plotted against him.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceC_219-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceC-219">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-heraldsun.com.au_220-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-heraldsun.com.au-220">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the lead up to the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Marriage_Law_Postal_Survey" title="Australian Marriage Law Postal Survey">2017 postal survey on same sex marriage</a>, Abbott campaigned for the retention of the status quo.<sup id="cite_ref-221" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-221">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup> During the campaign, Abbott was headbutted in Hobart by protester Astro Labe, who was sentenced to six months imprisonment.<sup id="cite_ref-222" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-222">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-223" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-223">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-224" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-224">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-225" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-225">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-226" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-226">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup> Following the "yes" vote in the plebiscite, Abbott said he accepted the result, and that the matter was settled.
</p><p>In October 2015, <a href="/wiki/The_Australian" title="The Australian">The Australian</a> reported that a "poll of 1631 voters shows 62 per cent of Australians believe the Liberal Party did the right thing" in ousting Abbott.<sup id="cite_ref-smh9dec_209-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-smh9dec-209">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-227" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-227">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup> Abbott was returned as the Member for Warringah at the subsequent election, but the Coalition's majority in the House of Representatives was reduced from 29 seats to one seat. In April 2018, the now-elected Turnbull government reached the 30-consecutive-Newspoll-losses benchmark Turnbull had used to unseat Abbott.<sup id="cite_ref-228" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-228">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> Turnbull resigned after losing the support of the Party in room during the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia_leadership_spills,_2018" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Party of Australia leadership spills, 2018">Liberal leadership spills of 2018</a>. Abbott supported <a href="/wiki/Peter_Dutton" title="Peter Dutton">Peter Dutton</a> in the leadership vote. Following the election of <a href="/wiki/Scott_Morrison" title="Scott Morrison">Scott Morrison</a> as party leader, Abbott said the Coalition now had a better chance of re-election in the upcoming poll, telling the <a href="/wiki/Centre_for_Independent_Studies" title="Centre for Independent Studies">Centre for Independent Studies</a>: "I am confident, given the ministerial appointments that he's made, that there will be better policy, there will be a united party, and there will be a sharper difference with our opponents."<sup id="cite_ref-229" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-229">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<dl><dt>Morrison government</dt></dl>
<p>Following Turnbull's resignation, Abbott was appointed as Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Scott_Morrison" title="Scott Morrison">Scott Morrison</a>'s Special Envoy on Indigenous Affairs, with a brief to focus on indigenous school attendance and performance.<sup id="cite_ref-230" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-230">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-231" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-231">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup> Abbott presented his first report to Parliament as Special Envoy in December 2018.<sup id="cite_ref-232" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-232">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup> He recommended increasing substantially the salary supplements and the retention bonuses for teachers in very remote areas; waiving HECS debt of longer term teachers in very remote schools; incentives for communities to adopt <a href="/wiki/Indue_card" class="mw-redirect" title="Indue card">debit card</a> arrangements; an extension of the Remote School Attendance Strategy, with more local school buy-in and engagement; extension of the Good to Great Schools program that has reintroduced phonics and disciplined learning for further evaluation and emulation; and that the government should match the Australian Indigenous Education Foundation's private and philanthropic funding on an ongoing basis.<sup id="cite_ref-233" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-233">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 18 May 2019, during the <a href="/wiki/2019_Australian_federal_election" title="2019 Australian federal election">federal election</a>, Abbott lost his seat of Warringah to independent candidate and former Olympic skier <a href="/wiki/Zali_Steggall" title="Zali Steggall">Zali Steggall</a>, marking the first time the seat had been lost by the Liberals and their predecessors since its creation in 1922.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Steggal ran as a pro-climate action candidate and her campaign won extensive media coverage and the backing of the left wing <a href="/wiki/GetUp!" title="GetUp!">GetUp!</a> lobby group. Abbott went into the election holding Warringah on a two party preferred margin of 61 percent. However, he lost over 12 percent of his primary vote from 2016, and finished over 4,100 votes behind Steggall on the first preference count and over 13,000 after preferences were distributed - losing by 57.2% to 42.8% .<sup id="cite_ref-234" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-234">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup> Abbott had won the seat at nine elections and served 25 years as the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Warringah" title="Division of Warringah">Member for Warringah</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-parliamentary_career">Post-parliamentary career</span></h2>
<p>On 4 September 2020, Abbott was appointed as an adviser to the UK's <a href="/wiki/Board_of_Trade" title="Board of Trade">Board of Trade</a> with the stated aim of providing "a range of views to help in its advisory function, promoting free and fair trade and advising on UK trade policy to the International Trade Secretary".<sup id="cite_ref-https&#58;//www.gov.uk/government/groups/board-of-trade#advisers-to-the-board-september-2020_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-https://www.gov.uk/government/groups/board-of-trade#advisers-to-the-board-september-2020-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> The role involves advising on the negotiation of international trade deals for the UK, but it was reported that Abbott would not be involved in advising the government on the country's <a href="/wiki/Brexit" title="Brexit">Brexit process</a>. He will be joined on the board by other senior political figures, including <a href="/wiki/Patricia_Hewitt" title="Patricia Hewitt">Patricia Hewitt</a>, a former UK <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Health" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State for Health">Secretary of State for Health</a>, <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Hannan" title="Daniel Hannan">Daniel Hannan</a>, a former <a href="/wiki/Member_of_the_European_Parliament" title="Member of the European Parliament">Member of the European Parliament</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Linda_Yueh" title="Linda Yueh">Linda Yueh</a>, a writer and broadcaster. News of the appointment prompted <a href="/wiki/Her_Majesty%27s_Most_Loyal_Opposition_(United_Kingdom)" class="mw-redirect" title="Her Majesty&#39;s Most Loyal Opposition (United Kingdom)">UK Opposition</a> politicians to question his suitability for the job because of comments previously made by Abbott about climate change, women and same-sex marriage. <a href="/wiki/Boris_Johnson" title="Boris Johnson">Boris Johnson</a>, the UK's Prime Minister, said that he could not agree with the views of everyone in his government, but that Abbott had been elected as Prime Minister by that "great, liberal democratic nation of Australia" which he said "speaks for itself".<sup id="cite_ref-235" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-235">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He currently holds the role of a Senior Advisor at <a href="/wiki/New_Direction_(think_tank)" title="New Direction (think tank)">New Direction</a>, a think tank affiliated with the <a href="/wiki/European_Conservatives_and_Reformists_Party" title="European Conservatives and Reformists Party">European Conservatives and Reformists Party</a> in the European Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-236" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-236">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In February 2023, Abbott joined the board of UK think-tank <a href="/wiki/Global_Warming_Policy_Foundation" class="mw-redirect" title="Global Warming Policy Foundation">Global Warming Policy Foundation</a> which is known for its <a href="/wiki/Climate_change_scepticism" class="mw-redirect" title="Climate change scepticism">climate change scepticism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-237" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-237">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Possible_Senate_appointment">Possible Senate appointment</span></h3>
<p>After the death of <a href="/wiki/Australian_Senate" title="Australian Senate">Senator</a> <a href="/wiki/Jim_Molan" title="Jim Molan">Jim Molan</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-238" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-238">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup> former <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia_(Victorian_Division)" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Party of Australia (Victorian Division)">Victorian Liberal</a> president <a href="/wiki/Michael_Kroger" title="Michael Kroger">Michael Kroger</a> in an interview with <a href="/wiki/Sky_News_Australia" title="Sky News Australia">Sky News Australia</a> suggested the idea of Abbott taking Molan's Senate seat.<sup id="cite_ref-239" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-239">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(Australia)" title="Leader of the Opposition (Australia)">Opposition Leader</a> Peter Dutton told <a href="/wiki/ABC_News_(Australia)" title="ABC News (Australia)">ABC News</a> that there was ''no question Tony Abbott would be an asset" but also stated that "there will be many other candidates who are very credible as well".<sup id="cite_ref-:3_240-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-240">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup> Abbott has not confirmed whether he is interested in returning to public life.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_240-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-240">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_views">Political views</span></h2>
<p>Abbott is a member of the <a href="/wiki/National_Right_(Liberal_Party_of_Australia)" title="National Right (Liberal Party of Australia)">National Right</a> faction of the Liberal Party.<sup id="cite_ref-241" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-241">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The term "Abbottism" has been coined by several media outlets to refer to his political ideology.<sup id="cite_ref-242" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-242">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-243" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-243">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-244" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-244">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-245" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-245">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-246" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-246">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup> Abbottism has been compared to several other <a href="/wiki/Eponym" title="Eponym">eponymous</a> political ideologies, namely <a href="/wiki/Hansonism" title="Hansonism">Hansonism</a> (the views of <a href="/wiki/Pauline_Hanson" title="Pauline Hanson">Pauline Hanson</a>)<sup id="cite_ref-247" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-247">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Trumpism" title="Trumpism">Trumpism</a> (the views of <a href="/wiki/Donald_Trump" title="Donald Trump">Donald Trump</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-248" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-248">&#91;248&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Aboriginal_affairs">Aboriginal affairs</span></h3>
<p>Abbott has an active interest in indigenous affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated8_249-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated8-249">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup> As Opposition Leader, Abbott promised to prioritise indigenous affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated8_249-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated8-249">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup> As Prime Minister, Abbott reformed the administration of the portfolio, moving it into the Department of Prime Minister.<sup id="cite_ref-250" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-250">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As Health Minister, Abbott established the Nurse Family Partnership to improve conditions for indigenous youth. As Opposition Leader, he worked with Cape York Aboriginal activist <a href="/wiki/Noel_Pearson" title="Noel Pearson">Noel Pearson</a>, volunteered as a teacher in remote Aboriginal Communities and gave a commitment to continue to live one week a year in such communities if elected Prime Minister.<sup id="cite_ref-251" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-251">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-252" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-252">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup> In contrast to his mentor John Howard, Abbott praised Rudd's National Apology to the Stolen Generation.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated5_89-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated5-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-eurekastreet1_90-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-eurekastreet1-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated6_91-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated6-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-253" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-253">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While the Coalition and Labor were engaged in negotiations with crossbenchers to obtain minority government in 2010, Noel Pearson lobbied <a href="/wiki/Rob_Oakeshott" title="Rob Oakeshott">Rob Oakeshott</a> to back Abbott.<sup id="cite_ref-254" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-254">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup> Rising to support the passage of the Gillard government's historic Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Peoples Recognition Bill through the House of Representatives in 2013, Abbott said:<sup id="cite_ref-255" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-255">&#91;255&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Australia is a blessed country. Our climate, our land, our people, our institutions rightly make us the envy of the earth, except for one thing—we have never fully made peace with the First Australians. This is the stain on our soul that Prime Minister Keating so movingly evoked at Redfern 21 years ago. We have to acknowledge that pre-1788 this land was as Aboriginal then as it is Australian now. Until we have acknowledged that we will be an incomplete nation and a torn people … So our challenge is to do now in these times what should have been done 200 or 100 years ago to acknowledge Aboriginal people in our country's foundation document. In short, we need to atone for the omissions and for the hardness of heart of our forebears to enable us all to embrace the future as a united people.</p></blockquote>
<p>In November 2012, Abbott flew to Alice Springs to back Aboriginal <a href="/wiki/Country_Liberal_Party" title="Country Liberal Party">Country Liberal Party</a> (CLP) MLA <a href="/wiki/Alison_Anderson" title="Alison Anderson">Alison Anderson</a> to run in the federal seat of <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Lingiari" title="Division of Lingiari">Lingiari</a> and to become the first Indigenous woman to enter Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-theaustralian1_256-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-theaustralian1-256">&#91;256&#93;</a></sup> Anderson eventually did not run as the CLP candidate for Lingiari in the <a href="/wiki/2013_Australian_federal_election" title="2013 Australian federal election">2013 federal election</a>.
</p><p>In August 2015, he disappointed Aboriginal leaders <a href="/wiki/Patrick_Dodson" class="mw-redirect" title="Patrick Dodson">Patrick Dodson</a> and <a href="/wiki/Noel_Pearson" title="Noel Pearson">Noel Pearson</a> by rejecting as potentially divisive their request for the federal government to fund a series of Indigenous-only conventions on the wording for the referendum.<sup id="cite_ref-257" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-257">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Constitutional_monarchy">Constitutional monarchy</span></h3>
<p>Abbott supports the <a href="/wiki/Monarchy_of_Australia" title="Monarchy of Australia">Australian monarchy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Four_Corners_258-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Four_Corners-258">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-259" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-259">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup> Before entering parliament, he worked as the Executive Director of <a href="/wiki/Australians_for_Constitutional_Monarchy" title="Australians for Constitutional Monarchy">Australians for Constitutional Monarchy</a> from 1993 to 1994.<sup id="cite_ref-tonyabbott.com.au_260-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tonyabbott.com.au-260">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In March 2014, Abbott advised the Queen to reintroduce the grade of Knight/Dame to the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Australia" title="Order of Australia">Order of Australia</a>, without discussing it in the Cabinet<sup id="cite_ref-Owens_2014_261-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Owens_2014-261">&#91;261&#93;</a></sup> and despite stating in December 2013 that he did not plan to do so.<sup id="cite_ref-Knott_2014_262-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Knott_2014-262">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Fraser_government" title="Fraser government">Fraser government</a> initially introduced the grade of Knight/Dame of the Order of Australia in 1976; the <a href="/wiki/Hawke_government" title="Hawke government">Hawke government</a> discontinued it in 1986.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Climate_change">Climate change</span></h3>
<p>Before becoming Opposition Leader, Abbott initially supported proposals by Liberal leaders Howard and Turnbull to introduce floating prices to reduce carbon emissions, but also <a href="/wiki/Climate_change_denial" title="Climate change denial">expressed some doubts</a> as to the science and economics underlying such initiatives. In 2009, Abbott announced his opposition to Turnbull's support for the Rudd government's Emissions Trading Scheme proposal, and successfully challenged Turnbull for the Liberal leadership, chiefly over this issue.<sup id="cite_ref-263" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-263">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-264" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-264">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup> As Opposition Leader, Abbott declared that he accepted that climate change was real and that humans were having an impact on it, but rejected <a href="/wiki/Carbon_pricing" class="mw-redirect" title="Carbon pricing">carbon pricing</a> as a means to address the issue, proposing instead to match the Labor government's 5% emissions reduction target through implementation of a plan involving financial incentives for emissions reductions by industry, and support for carbon storage in soils and expanded forests.<sup id="cite_ref-265" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-265">&#91;265&#93;</a></sup> On the eve of the 2013 election, Abbott told the ABC:<sup id="cite_ref-266" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-266">&#91;266&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>[J]ust to make it clear... I think that climate change is real, humanity makes a contribution. It's important to take strong and effective action against it, and that is what our direct action policy does. … The important thing is to take strong and effective action to tackle climate change, action that doesn't damage our economy. And that is why the incentive-based system that we've got, the direct action policies, which are quite similar to those that president <a href="/wiki/Barack_Obama" title="Barack Obama">Obama</a> has put into practice, is – that's the smart way to deal with this, a big tax is a dumb way to deal with it.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite>Abbott on <a href="/wiki/ABC_TV_(Australian_TV_channel)" title="ABC TV (Australian TV channel)">ABC TV</a> <i>Insiders</i> prior to 2013 election.</cite></div></blockquote>
<p>Before becoming Opposition Leader in November 2009, Abbott questioned the science of climate change and an ETS.  In November 2009, Abbott outlined his objections to the Rudd government's carbon pricing plan on the ABC's Lateline program.  Upon becoming Leader of the Opposition, Abbott put the question of support for the Government's <a href="/wiki/Carbon_Pollution_Reduction_Scheme" title="Carbon Pollution Reduction Scheme">Carbon Pollution Reduction Scheme</a> (CPRS) to a secret ballot and the Liberal Party voted to reject the policy – overturning an undertaking by Turnbull to support an amended version of the government's scheme. Under Abbott, the Coalition joined the Greens and voted against the CPRS in the Senate, and the bill was defeated twice, providing a <a href="/wiki/Double_dissolution" title="Double dissolution">double dissolution</a> trigger.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_267-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-267">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup> The Rudd government eventually deferred its CPRS legislation until 2013.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceB_267-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceB-267">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With Abbott as Opposition Leader, the Liberal party opposed a carbon emissions tax and an Emissions Trading Scheme.<sup id="cite_ref-y7libparty2010_268-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-y7libparty2010-268">&#91;268&#93;</a></sup> Abbott predicted in March 2012 that the Gillard government's carbon tax would be the world's "biggest".<sup id="cite_ref-269" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-269">&#91;269&#93;</a></sup> A January 2013 OECD report on taxation of energy use measured Australia's effective tax rate on carbon at 1 July 2012 as among the lower rates in the OECD.<sup id="cite_ref-270" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-270">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup> In July 2011, Abbott criticised the proposed powers of the government's carbon tax regulator.<sup id="cite_ref-271" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-271">&#91;271&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-272" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-272">&#91;272&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-273" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-273">&#91;273&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 2017, Abbott spoke in London at the <a href="/wiki/Global_Warming_Policy_Foundation" class="mw-redirect" title="Global Warming Policy Foundation">Global Warming Policy Foundation</a>, a climate-skeptic lobby group, where he described climate change as "probably doing good; or at least, more good than harm."<sup id="cite_ref-:0_274-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-274">&#91;274&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:1_275-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-275">&#91;275&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-276" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-276">&#91;276&#93;</a></sup> He argued that higher concentrations of carbon dioxide act as "plant food" and "are actually greening the planet and helping to lift agricultural yields."<sup id="cite_ref-:0_274-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-274">&#91;274&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:1_275-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-275">&#91;275&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 15 December 2019, he claimed that the world was "in the grip of a climate cult".<sup id="cite_ref-277" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-277">&#91;277&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Social_policy">Social policy</span></h3>
<p>Abbott opposed the legislation of <a href="/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_Australia" title="Same-sex marriage in Australia">same-sex marriage in Australia</a>.  Abbott is an opponent of <a href="/wiki/Embryonic_stem_cell_research" class="mw-redirect" title="Embryonic stem cell research">embryonic stem cell research</a> and <a href="/wiki/Euthanasia" title="Euthanasia">euthanasia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-278" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-278">&#91;278&#93;</a></sup> He supports the right for women to have an <a href="/wiki/Spontaneous_abortion" class="mw-redirect" title="Spontaneous abortion">abortion</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Four_Corners_258-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Four_Corners-258">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-279" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-279">&#91;279&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-280" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-280">&#91;280&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-281" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-281">&#91;281&#93;</a></sup> As Health Minister, he tried, but failed, to block the introduction of the abortion pill <a href="/wiki/RU-486" class="mw-redirect" title="RU-486">RU-486</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-282" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-282">&#91;282&#93;</a></sup> As Health Minister, Abbott advocated for reducing the <a href="/wiki/Abortion_in_Australia" title="Abortion in Australia">number of abortions performed each year</a> as a national priority, and referred to abortion as the "easy way out".<sup id="cite_ref-283" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-283">&#91;283&#93;</a></sup> Abbott opposed allowing the introduction of embryonic stem cell research or <i><a href="/wiki/Therapeutic_cloning" class="mw-redirect" title="Therapeutic cloning">therapeutic cloning</a></i> in a <a href="/wiki/Conscience_vote" title="Conscience vote">conscience vote</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Cloning_laws_284-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cloning_laws-284">&#91;284&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his 2009 book <i>Battlelines</i>, Abbott proposed that consideration should be given to a return to an optional <a href="/wiki/At-fault_divorce" class="mw-redirect" title="At-fault divorce">at-fault divorce</a> agreement between couples who would like it, similar to the <i>Matrimonial Causes Act</i>, which would require spouses to prove offences like <a href="/wiki/Adultery" title="Adultery">adultery</a>, habitual drunkenness, cruelty, desertion, or a five-year separation before a divorce would be granted.<sup id="cite_ref-285" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-285">&#91;285&#93;</a></sup> Abbott said that this would be a way of "providing additional recognition to what might be thought of as traditional marriage".<sup id="cite_ref-286" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-286">&#91;286&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Early on in his Prime Ministership, the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Capital_Territory_Legislative_Assembly" title="Australian Capital Territory Legislative Assembly">Australian Capital Territory Legislative Assembly</a> passed the <a href="/wiki/Marriage_Equality_(Same_Sex)_Act_2013" title="Marriage Equality (Same Sex) Act 2013">Marriage Equality (Same Sex) Act 2013</a>, a bill to allow same-sex couples to legally marry.<sup id="cite_ref-287" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-287">&#91;287&#93;</a></sup> Abbott announced that the federal government would challenge this decision in the <a href="/wiki/High_Court_of_Australia" title="High Court of Australia">High Court</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-288" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-288">&#91;288&#93;</a></sup> The case was heard on 3 December. Nine days later, on 12 December, the High Court gave judgement that the Same Sex Act would be dismantled as it clashed with the Federal Marriage Act 1961.<sup id="cite_ref-289" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-289">&#91;289&#93;</a></sup> When the <a href="/wiki/Marriage_Amendment_(Definition_and_Religious_Freedoms)_Act_2017" title="Marriage Amendment (Definition and Religious Freedoms) Act 2017">Marriage Amendment (Definition and Religious Freedoms) Act 2017</a>, which posed the question of whether same-sex couples should be able to marry, was presented to members of parliament, Abbott abstained from voting.<sup id="cite_ref-290" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-290">&#91;290&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-291" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-291">&#91;291&#93;</a></sup> Ultimately, same-sex marriage would be passed into law in December 2017.<sup id="cite_ref-292" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-292">&#91;292&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Abbott supported <a href="/wiki/Peter_Dutton" title="Peter Dutton">Peter Dutton</a>'s call to give "special treatment" to white <a href="/wiki/White_South_Africans" title="White South Africans">South African</a> farmers seeking asylum.<sup id="cite_ref-293" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-293">&#91;293&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-294" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-294">&#91;294&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="National_Broadband_Network">National Broadband Network</span></h3>
<p>Abbott was opposed to a majority <a href="/wiki/Fiber_to_the_x#FTTP" title="Fiber to the x">Fibre-to-the-Home</a> (FttH) <a href="/wiki/National_Broadband_Network" title="National Broadband Network">National Broadband Network</a> (NBN). In 2010, as Leader of the Opposition, Abbott stated that he would "ferociously" hold the Labor government to account over what he believed to be "a white elephant on a massive scale" and would "demolish" the NBN.<sup id="cite_ref-295" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-295">&#91;295&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-296" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-296">&#91;296&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2010, Abbott argued that an <a href="/wiki/LTE_(telecommunication)" title="LTE (telecommunication)">LTE</a> network could meet Australia's future broadband needs, with "a tower on every street corner".<sup id="cite_ref-297" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-297">&#91;297&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2011, he called for the NBN to be scrapped entirely with funding diverted to assist with recovery efforts following the Queensland floods, stating "The National Broadband Network is a luxury that Australia cannot now afford. The one thing you don't do is redo your bathroom when your roof has just been blown off."<sup id="cite_ref-298" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-298">&#91;298&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With Malcolm Turnbull as Shadow Minister for Communications and Broadband, the Liberal/National Coalition proposed an alternative - The Multi-Technology Mix (MTM), which heavily utilised <a href="/wiki/Fiber_to_the_x#FTTN_and_FTTC" title="Fiber to the x">Fibre to the Node</a> (FttN) technology - in the lead up to the 2013 Australian federal election. Abbott said that if elected, all Australians would have access to a minimum broadband speed of 25Mbit/s by the end of their first term of government. They promised download speeds between 25 and 100 Mbit/s by the end of 2016 and 50 to 100 Mbit/s by 2019, with the rollout completed by the end of 2019.<sup id="cite_ref-299" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-299">&#91;299&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="China_and_Taiwan">China and Taiwan</span></h3>
<p>Abbott called the <a href="/wiki/AUKUS" title="AUKUS">AUKUS</a> defense pact between the United States, the United Kingdom, and Australia, which is directed at countering <a href="/wiki/China" title="China">Chinese</a> power in the <a href="/wiki/Indo-Pacific" title="Indo-Pacific">Indo-Pacific</a> region, "the biggest decision that any Australian government has made in decades" as "it indicates that we are going to stand shoulder to shoulder with the United States and the United Kingdom in meeting the great strategic challenge of our time, which obviously, is China".<sup id="cite_ref-abcr_300-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-abcr-300">&#91;300&#93;</a></sup> Abbott said that Australia would be safer as a result, and cited China's increasing naval firepower as a justification for the deal.<sup id="cite_ref-abcr_300-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-abcr-300">&#91;300&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Abbott strongly supports the <i><a href="/wiki/De_jure" title="De jure">de jure</a></i> <a href="/wiki/International_recognition_of_Taiwan" class="mw-redirect" title="International recognition of Taiwan">recognition</a> of <a href="/wiki/Taiwan" title="Taiwan">Taiwan</a> as an independent nation. In 2021, Abbott attended a regional forum hosted in <a href="/wiki/Taipei" title="Taipei">Taipei</a> and met with <a href="/wiki/President_of_Taiwan" class="mw-redirect" title="President of Taiwan">Taiwanese President</a> <a href="/wiki/Tsai_Ing-wen" title="Tsai Ing-wen">Tsai Ing-wen</a>. During a speech, he stated that he wants to help Taiwan end its isolation from global affairs and reaffirmed Australia's solidarity with the country amid increasing tensions with China.<sup id="cite_ref-301" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-301">&#91;301&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-302" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-302">&#91;302&#93;</a></sup> In the speech, he also referred to China as a "bully" and <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_People%27s_Republic_of_China" title="President of the People&#39;s Republic of China">Chinese President</a> <a href="/wiki/Xi_Jinping" title="Xi Jinping">Xi Jinping</a> as "the new red emperor".<sup id="cite_ref-303" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-303">&#91;303&#93;</a></sup> Chinese officials highly criticised Abbott's visit and speech,<sup id="cite_ref-304" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-304">&#91;304&#93;</a></sup> although Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Scott_Morrison" title="Scott Morrison">Scott Morrison</a> and <a href="/wiki/Morrison_government" title="Morrison government">his government</a> defended his visit.<sup id="cite_ref-305" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-305">&#91;305&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span></h2>
<p>In March 2020, an Australian <a href="/wiki/Security_hacker" title="Security hacker">computer security researcher</a> obtained Abbott's passport number and personal phone number after Abbott posted a photo of his aeroplane <a href="/wiki/Boarding_pass" title="Boarding pass">boarding pass</a> on Instagram. The researcher found a <a href="/wiki/Vulnerability_(computing)" title="Vulnerability (computing)">security flaw</a> in the online check-in portal of the airline carrier Qantas, that divulged sensitive information given details printed on the pass.<sup id="cite_ref-306" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-306">&#91;306&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-307" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-307">&#91;307&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-308" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-308">&#91;308&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-309" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-309">&#91;309&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Abbott has admitted to having smoked <a href="/wiki/Cannabis" title="Cannabis">cannabis</a> once.<sup id="cite_ref-310" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-310">&#91;310&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Marriage_and_children">Marriage and children</span></h3>
<p>When Abbott was 22, his girlfriend at the time became pregnant and claimed he was the biological father. The couple did not marry and put the child up for adoption. For 27 years, Abbott believed that he was the father of the child.<sup id="cite_ref-311" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-311">&#91;311&#93;</a></sup> In 2004, the man sought out Abbott, and it was publicly revealed he was an <a href="/wiki/Australian_Broadcasting_Corporation" title="Australian Broadcasting Corporation">ABC</a> sound recordist who worked in <a href="/wiki/Parliament_House,_Canberra" title="Parliament House, Canberra">Parliament House, Canberra</a>, and was involved in making television programs in which Abbott appeared.<sup id="cite_ref-Abbott_reunited_312-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Abbott_reunited-312">&#91;312&#93;</a></sup> The story was reported around the world, but <a href="/wiki/DNA_testing" class="mw-redirect" title="DNA testing">DNA testing</a> later revealed that Abbott was not the man's father.<sup id="cite_ref-DNA_Test_313-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DNA_Test-313">&#91;313&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Margie_Abbott_and_Tony_Abbott_at_the_Canberra_Operation_Slipper_Welcome_Home_Ceremony_in_March_2015.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Margie_Abbott_and_Tony_Abbott_at_the_Canberra_Operation_Slipper_Welcome_Home_Ceremony_in_March_2015.jpg/170px-Margie_Abbott_and_Tony_Abbott_at_the_Canberra_Operation_Slipper_Welcome_Home_Ceremony_in_March_2015.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="226" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Margie_Abbott_and_Tony_Abbott_at_the_Canberra_Operation_Slipper_Welcome_Home_Ceremony_in_March_2015.jpg/255px-Margie_Abbott_and_Tony_Abbott_at_the_Canberra_Operation_Slipper_Welcome_Home_Ceremony_in_March_2015.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Margie_Abbott_and_Tony_Abbott_at_the_Canberra_Operation_Slipper_Welcome_Home_Ceremony_in_March_2015.jpg/340px-Margie_Abbott_and_Tony_Abbott_at_the_Canberra_Operation_Slipper_Welcome_Home_Ceremony_in_March_2015.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1957" data-file-height="2605" /></a><figcaption>Margie and Tony Abbott in 2015</figcaption></figure>
<p>Following his departure from the seminary, Abbott met and married <a href="/wiki/Margie_Abbott" title="Margie Abbott">Margaret "Margie" Aitken</a>, a New Zealander working in Sydney.<sup id="cite_ref-314" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-314">&#91;314&#93;</a></sup> The couple have three daughters (Louise, Bridget and Frances),<sup id="cite_ref-dailytelegraph1_29-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dailytelegraph1-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-partypage_315-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-partypage-315">&#91;315&#93;</a></sup> and became grandparents in 2021.<sup id="cite_ref-316" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-316">&#91;316&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span></h3>
<p>Abbott is a <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic">Roman Catholic</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-317" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-317">&#91;317&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-318" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-318">&#91;318&#93;</a></sup> Before the 2013 Election, Abbott spoke of his religious outlook:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>The Jesuits helped to instill in me this thought that our calling in life was to be … 'a man for others' … I am a pretty traditional Catholic... I'm not an evangelical, a charismatic Christian, I'm not. I try to attend Mass, but I don't get there every Sunday any more... Faith has certainly helped to shape my life, but it doesn't in any way determine my politics".</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite>Tony Abbott on ABC TV's <i>Kitchen Cabinet</i>; September 2013.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated7_48-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated7-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup></cite></div></blockquote>
<p>As a former Catholic <a href="/wiki/Seminarian" class="mw-redirect" title="Seminarian">seminarian</a>, Abbott's <a href="/wiki/Religiosity" title="Religiosity">religiosity</a> has come to national attention and journalists have often sought his views on the role of <a href="/wiki/Religion_and_politics" class="mw-redirect" title="Religion and politics">religion in politics</a>. According to <a href="/wiki/John_Warhurst_(academic)" title="John Warhurst (academic)">John Warhurst</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_National_University" title="Australian National University">Australian National University</a>, academics have at times placed an "exaggerated concentration on the religious affiliation and personal religious background of just one of [the Howard government's] senior ministers, Tony Abbott."<sup id="cite_ref-warhurst_319-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-warhurst-319">&#91;319&#93;</a></sup> Journalist <a href="/wiki/Michelle_Grattan" title="Michelle Grattan">Michelle Grattan</a> wrote in 2010 that while Abbott has always "worn his Catholicism on his sleeve", he is "clearly frustrated by the obsession with [it] and what might hang off that".<sup id="cite_ref-grattan_320-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-grattan-320">&#91;320&#93;</a></sup> Abbott has said that a politician should not rely on religion to justify a political point of view:<sup id="cite_ref-Four_Corners_258-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Four_Corners-258">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>We are all influenced by a value system that we hold, but in the end, every decision that a politician makes is, or at least should, in our society be based on the normal sorts of considerations. It's got to be publicly justifiable; not only justifiable in accordance with a private view; a private belief.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite>Abbott on <a href="/wiki/ABC_TV_(Australian_TV_channel)" title="ABC TV (Australian TV channel)">ABC TV</a> <i>Four Corners</i>, March 2010.</cite></div></blockquote>
<p>Various political positions supported by Abbott have been criticised by church representatives, including aspects of Coalition industrial relations, asylum seeker, and Aboriginal affairs policies.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2007_321-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2007-321">&#91;321&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-322" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-322">&#91;322&#93;</a></sup> After criticisms of Liberal Party policy by clergy, Abbott has said, "The priesthood gives someone the power to consecrate bread and wine into the <a href="/wiki/Transubstantiation" title="Transubstantiation">body and blood of Christ</a>. It doesn't give someone the power to convert poor logic into good logic."<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2007_321-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2007-321">&#91;321&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Community_service">Community service</span></h2>
<p>Abbott is an active volunteer member for the <a href="/wiki/Davidson,_New_South_Wales" title="Davidson, New South Wales">Davidson</a>, <a href="/wiki/New_South_Wales_Rural_Fire_Service" title="New South Wales Rural Fire Service">NSW Rural Fire Service</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Bushfires_323-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bushfires-323">&#91;323&#93;</a></sup> He is also an active volunteer member of the <a href="/wiki/Queenscliff,_New_South_Wales" title="Queenscliff, New South Wales">Queenscliff</a> <a href="/wiki/Surf_Life_Saving_Club" title="Surf Life Saving Club">Surf Life Saving Club</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Liberal_CV_324-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Liberal_CV-324">&#91;324&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Abbott participates in the Pollie Pedal, an annual 1,000&#160;km charity bike ride. In April 2007, he launched the tenth annual Pollie Pedal, to raise money for breast cancer research.<sup id="cite_ref-Charity_Ride_325-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Charity_Ride-325">&#91;325&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2008, Abbott spent three weeks teaching in a remote <a href="/wiki/Aboriginal_Australian" class="mw-redirect" title="Aboriginal Australian">Aboriginal</a> settlement in <a href="/wiki/Coen,_Queensland" title="Coen, Queensland">Coen</a> on Cape York, organised through Indigenous leader <a href="/wiki/Noel_Pearson" title="Noel Pearson">Noel Pearson</a>. He taught remedial reading to Aboriginal children and worked with an income management group helping families manage their welfare payments. In 2009, he spent 10 days in <a href="/wiki/Aurukun" class="mw-redirect" title="Aurukun">Aurukun</a> on Cape York working with the truancy team, visiting children who had not been attending school. Abbott's stated goal for these visits was to familiarise himself with <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_Australian" class="mw-redirect" title="Indigenous Australian">Indigenous</a> issues.<sup id="cite_ref-326" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-326">&#91;326&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-327" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-327">&#91;327&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Writings">Writings</span></h2>
<p>Abbott has published four books. In 2009, he launched <i>Battlelines</i>; a personal biography, reflections on the <a href="/wiki/Howard_government" title="Howard government">Howard government</a> and discussion of potential policy directions for the Liberal Party of Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-tonyabbott.com.au_260-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tonyabbott.com.au-260">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup> Previously he had published two books in defence of the existing <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy" title="Constitutional monarchy">constitutional monarchy</a> system, <i>The Minimal Monarchy</i> and <i>How to Win the Constitutional War</i>. In 2012, he released a compilation of key speeches from that year, entitled <i>A Strong Australia</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Ireland,_Judith_161-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ireland,_Judith-161">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="National">National</span></h3>
<ul><li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/Australia" title="Australia"><img alt="Australia" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/23px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/35px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/46px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="640" /></a></span></span> <b>8 June 2020</b> <a href="/wiki/Companion_of_the_Order_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Companion of the Order of Australia">Companion of the Order of Australia</a> (AC), For eminent service to the people and Parliament of Australia, particularly as Prime Minister, and through significant contributions to trade, border control, and to the Indigenous community.<sup id="cite_ref-328" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-328">&#91;328&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/Australia" title="Australia"><img alt="Australia" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/23px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/35px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/46px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="640" /></a></span></span> <b>4 November 2018</b> <a href="/wiki/National_Medal_(Australia)" title="National Medal (Australia)">National Medal (Australia)</a>, for 15 years service as a volunteer Firefighter.<sup id="cite_ref-329" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-329">&#91;329&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/Australia" title="Australia"><img alt="Australia" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/23px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/35px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/88/Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg/46px-Flag_of_Australia_%28converted%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="640" /></a></span></span> <b>1 January 2001</b> <a href="/wiki/Centenary_Medal" title="Centenary Medal">Centenary Medal</a>, for service as Minister for Employment and Workplace Relations.<sup id="cite_ref-330" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-330">&#91;330&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
